## REVIEW

# O'Connor movement review

Read the complete supplied movement in chapter order before judging it. State
whether this is self-review, a fresh-context simulated cold read, or a real human
read. Do not claim independent testing when you have the drafting history.

First give an unscored reader response: where interest caught, where it slipped,
which person matters, which event you expect next, and whether you would continue.
Then use two perspectives: a fluent thirteen-year-old reader and an adult genre
reader. These are lenses, not demographic research. Do not infer a sales forecast.

For each applicable dimension give a 1–10 score, an exact passage location, a reason
and confidence. Mark an absent feature N/A instead of giving invented evidence:

| Dimension | What to judge |
|---|---|
| Opening pull | A concrete reason to care before explanation takes over |
| Keep reading | Unresolved pressure and satisfying local payoff |
| Interest / freshness | Specific people, choices and problems |
| Clarity / flow | Meaning on one pass and clean transitions |
| Character attachment | Agency, vulnerability, recognizable differences |
| Humor / warmth | Character-grounded pleasure, timing, emotional honesty |
| Action / suspense | Trackable contest, adaptation, cost, earned scene length |
| Progression payoff | Effort visibly changes capability and consequence |
| Connection | State, plants and relationships carried across chapters |
| Read-aloud quality | Punctuation, referents, dialogue attribution and cadence |

Anchor interpretation: 5 = understandable but inconsistent; 7 = engaging with
located weaknesses; 9 = compelling with few substantial distractions. These are
editorial judgments, not calibrated measurements. Do not average the two readers
into a release gate. Do not award points for matching another book's word counts.

Separately check canon, physical state, power limits, knowledge boundaries and
reserved disclosures. Cite the project evidence for a contradiction. If only the
plan supports a claim, identify it as plan adherence rather than a canon error.

Return a concise repair brief with at most three priorities. Each needs location,
observed issue, effect on the reader, proposed scope and a strength to preserve.
Retain the developed action the owner requested. Punctuation can repair an unclear
sentence; do not shorten a successful fight merely to reduce its word count.

If comparing runs, keep chapter coverage and scoring rubric equal. Hide model and
seat labels from a fresh reader where feasible. Report tradeoffs and uncertainty;
one movement does not prove which seat is better across a series.

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-13.md

# Chapter 13 — Ours

The page lay where it had lain all night, on its square of oilcloth at the bare end of the deal, and the first grey light out of the one small window showed Cael that nobody had moved it.

He had slept beside it, after a fashion, on the meal-sacks. He had got up twice in the dark to see that it was there, which was foolish, since a sheet of desk paper does not walk; and each time he had stood over it, keeping both hands by him, and had gone back to the sacks.

Karis came in before the smith's first hammer, with her gloves on already.

"I am packing," she said. "I said that I should count in daylight, on a table without elbows, and I have been awake since the middle of the night working out where such a table is to be had. It is not in this inn. So everything goes into the flat, in the order that he gave it, and the flat goes where I go."

She folded the oilcloth over the page along its own creases, and did not look at him while she did it. The broker's sheet went under the sixth row, and the straps drew down across a red stamp which said that the thing was to go by hand.

"You have still not touched it."

"No."

"I am not asking you to. I only enter what I see." She put the flat under her arm. "It is a hand's width that you have kept, all night. I should not have had the patience."

He followed her out through the tap-room, where the keeper was raking his ashes, and into the yard.

It was the two hundred and seventy-eighth morning past the Line, and cold, with a white mist on the river that hid the cable's middle. Under the long eave, on twelve feet of tarred oak, Pike had his book open and his stone on the leaf. He was driving a tack with the butt of his penknife into the last clear space at the plank's right hand.

"That came over in the bag," he said, without turning. "I had it yesterday, with eleven pieces to give out and a boat that nearly went down to the mill. I don't pin by lamp."

Five of them read it together from the left-hand post, since Lira and Brom had come round from the well, and Seln had been standing there already, with no wand under his arm. It was a strip of bark, like every other slip on that oak, in the square black hand. Beside the words Pike had drawn a little road going between two heaps, and on the road something with four legs.

*Clearance. The cut below the shoulder hold, on the border road, two days west.*

"It's twenty," said Pike. "At the hold's own gate, on the factor's thumb, when the factor's walked it. That's a hold that's lost carts. It doesn't say what to. They never do; they'd have to pay more."

Nobody answered him at once.

Cael read the slip a second time, as he read anything, and it was the last five words that he read. Two roads left the western landing. One ran away to the south and west for three hundred miles, to a ford and a station and a house with a seal, and an old man had come down it on a stick. The other was the border road. It went up over a moor to the Line by the short way, and Cael knew what stood at the end of it, since a small pale woman had laid her palm on a strip of oilcloth and told him; and last night he had read it again, in a square black copy, under a title.

He did not need to look round to know that the other four were reading the same five words.

"Two days west," said Lira, with her lips. She did not say the rest.

At the far end of the eave, beyond the wash-house door, the keeper had a settle against the wall for carters to wait on. Vastin sat there now, at the door end, with his stiff leg out before him and both hands on the head of the ash stick. He was on no slip on that oak, and on no line of that book. He had read the new one as soon as it was pinned; Cael saw his eyes go to it once and then away to the river, as a guest's go from a letter that is not his.

"Ours," said Karis.

She had said it small and clear, with the flat under her arm. Cael had known her since a library table in Ostrand, and in all that time he had never once heard her claim a job. She had put her hand up for fences and for roofs when hands were asked for, and had gone where the count sent her; she had never looked at a slip and wanted it. Pike's pen stopped over the horn.

"Ours," said Brom.

He had said that word at this plank before, of a fence, and had had it written down. He said it now as he would have set his foot on a plank to learn whether it bore. Then he looked at Cael, and so did the other three.

---

"You said hands," said Lira. "At the plank, in daylight."

"I did."

"Then say what they're for. I'll not put mine up for a thing with two meanings."

It was fair, and he had lain on the meal-sacks for most of a night finding out that he could answer it.

"They're for a slip. There's a cut two days up a road, with something in it that takes carts, and a hold that'll pay twenty to have it out. I can price that. I know what a clearance costs; each of you has a mark on you from the last one." He kept his eyes on the bark. "I'm not ruling on a town. I said that I'd not rule over a thing I hadn't touched, and I've still not touched it. I can't price a town. Every figure that I've got for it is somebody else's."

"It's the same road," said Brom.

"It's the same road, and I can read. I'm not going to stand at this post and pretend that I took a job without seeing where it lay, and I'd not ask any of you to pretend it either." He made himself look at them then: a big man with four yards of webbing on him, and a girl with her left hand in her coat pocket, and a small pale woman holding six years of somebody's counting under her arm. "The boxes come. So do the papers, and the kit. I'll not leave a thing of ours in a store for a week with a river between. When the cut's done we'll be two days nearer than we are this morning, with the road going on in front of us, and I'll know more about what I'm walking with than I do now. That's all that I can honestly say I've decided. It's a job. I'll not make it a vow."

"That is a ruling," said Seln.

"It's half of one."

"It is the half that can be kept. I have seen a great many of the other kind." He had his hands in his sleeves. "I should sooner follow a man up a road for twenty marks than for a principle. The twenty marks will still be there in the morning."

Lira had been considering Cael with her head on one side.

"I told you so last night. You said that you'd ask for hands, and I knew what you'd ask them for before you'd shut your mouth. You were never going to say the word. You were going to find a slip." She took her left hand out of her pocket. "I'm not complaining. It's how I'd have done it. I only want it known that I saw it first."

"It's known."

"Then hands," said Lira, and put up the left.

It went up slowly, and a little crooked at the elbow, and it shook. But it was the first time since a white floor on the Fallow that she had voted with it, and she held it there until he had seen. Brom's left went up beside it. Karis shifted the flat, and raised her right hand with the glove still on. Seln lifted two fingers, and Cael put up his own.

"Five," said Pike. He made the strokes at the slip's foot, and then he did not dip his pen again. He turned his shoulder to them. "And who's your healer?"

"We've not—"

"It's a clearance. I've written a good many of those taken, and I've written some of them closed with a line drawn under a name. In my second year here a crew of four went up the north water, and one of them bled for two days on a hurdle for want of anybody who knew where to put a thumb. Since then I don't write a clearance taken until I've a healer said. It's not the hold's rule. It's mine." He scratched his stump. "Teague's old woman goes up the cold road with him. So who's yours?"

Nobody at the post said the name. They had laid a sixth bowl for it every night since a heap of stones, and had seen it eaten out of standing; and not one of the five would have named her to a ledger without her leave.

"I can hear you not saying it."

Oryn was in the wash-house door, a yard past the plank's end, drying her hands a finger at a time. There were four people on her bench already. She looked along them, and at the river; and last, for the length of a breath, she looked toward the settle at the eave's far end, where a stiff leg was stretched out on the flags.

"I've a bench here that's done by noon, and it's had six mornings of me. The mill's my second stop. It's waited a whole cold term, and it can wait four days more while I go round by the top and come down on it from above; I've five gates up that way that I'd have had to climb to in any case." She threw the cloth over her shoulder. "I said once that somebody ought to stand at an edge with her sleeves up. I'm not having a girl with a fortnight's arm go into a cut behind something that takes carts, and hearing about it afterward from a carter. Write me. I'm a line on that slip. I'm not a name under theirs."

Pike considered her in his double fashion. Six days ago he had had no word in his book for a woman who did what nobody had posted.

"That's posted," he said slowly. "A clearance wants a healer. It's my own rule, and you've answered it, and I can write posted." The pen went into the horn. Beneath the five strokes he made a sixth line, apart from them, and said it over twice to the mist. "*And a healer; of her own road; at her own price.* That's the first time I've had a column for you."

"Don't grow fond of it," said Oryn, and went in to her bench.

---

They were on the landing an hour before noon, with the mist gone and the river a hand lower than it had been at dusk.

It took that long to leave a place where five people had lain for a hundred and forty-eight nights. Brom carried the three boxes down from the hearth's end one at a time, roped as he had roped them in the night, and slung them on the brown mule with the beckets outward, while she stood for it at the shed's door with her ears back, like a person being measured for a coat. Karis walked beside the load with the hide under one arm and the flat under the other, and would give neither of them to the mule.

Cael left the word with Pike, as he had promised a better captain that he would: the cut, two days west by the border road, and whatever lay beyond it, with no day put on the coming back. Pike wrote nothing. He said that he had a season line of theirs with no number against it, and had stopped expecting to fill it by the week.

Vastin had risen from the settle when the first box went by.

He did not come to the plank, and he asked nobody anything. Cael saw him go in to the keeper, and come out again putting up his small purse; he had paid for the second night in the loft, and it appeared that he did not mean to have a third. He took up his pack by its one sound strap. Then he stood by the gate with the stick grounded until the mule had gone through it, and Oryn after the mule, with her strip of canvas rolled under her arm. When the last of them was a dozen strides down the bank he came after, at his own pace, and kept that distance.

Nobody had asked him, and Cael noticed that nobody turned round to tell him no.

Teague's salt was going down the planks. There were forty sacks of it in a grey heap at the landing's head, and the ferryman would have no cart on his boat yet, or any bale that a man could not carry; and so it went aboard on backs. The bow took a sack at a time, and came up again easing his weight onto the right leg. The boy took two, and was told by nobody to take one. The tall man carried his on the shoulder that the spear did not use, with seven feet of ash in his other hand, shod with its steel again, and he set each sack down in the scow's waist square upon the last, as though he were laying a course of stone.

Teague stood at the post's foot with a slip in his fist, counting them by. He glanced at the mule and the three boxes, and it was enough for him.

"Which way?"

"West. There's a cut below a hold, two days up the border road, that's been taking carts. It's ours."

"The shoulder hold. I've carried salt up to them. It's a bad piece of road in the cold; it lies in its own shadow, and there's water comes down the middle of it. I never lost a mule there, since I went through at noon, on foot, with their heads held." His eyes had gone past Cael's shoulder, up the bank, to where a brown coat was coming down a stride at a time. They stayed there a stride longer than a crew looks, as they had the morning before, on a pair of hands on the head of a stick, wrapped now in strips of the keeper's linen. "He's walking with you."

"He's walking behind us. I've not asked him, and I've not told him no."

"A man that holds a thing so doesn't leave off because nobody's asked him. I'd know what I had at my back, if it were my road."

It was as near as he had ever come to giving advice, and Cael took it as he would have taken a touch called. He did not say that he knew exactly what was at his back. He had sat across four feet of table from it, once, for three days.

"My term's where I left it," said Teague.

"I know where it is."

The chin came up by the width of a finger. Then he turned to his sacks. The tall man went by with another on his shoulder, and gave five people and a healer the length of a stride, and not a word.

The ferryman took the mule aboard last, with a sack over her eyes.

"A copper a head, and she's a head." He looked at the boxes. "They're not. They're on her."

Brom counted eight coppers out of the small money into that long sour palm. Then he looked at what he had done, and took one back.

"He pays his own," he said, low, to Cael. "He told us so at the board. I'd not shame him."

Vastin had his copper ready. He gave it with the short stiff bow, and stepped over the rail bad leg first, and went aft without being told, to the post where a new strap of stern line had been rove. He stood there through the whole of the crossing with his hands on his stick, a foot from the thing that he had held two days before with his back, and did not touch it. The ferryman, at the sweep, looked once at him and once at the strap. Cael thought that it was the nearest that man had ever come to thanking anybody.

---

Above the western landing a long stack lay under tarred sheets, as it had lain since the last boat of the old year.

Brom had the purse in his fist before the mule was off the scow. He had carried that debt since before the snow: sixty yards of four-strand rope, to a ferryman who would take no silver for it. A brisk man with ink on his cuffs came along the stack to meet him, with a clerk at his elbow.

"Cordage," said Brom. "Sixty yards. Four-strand."

"I've the very thing."

The broker turned back a corner of the sheet himself, and laid his hand on the nearest coil as a man lays his hand on a horse that he means to sell. Brom did not look at the tag; he took the rope's end between his finger and thumb, and bent it back on itself, and opened the lay with his nail.

"That's three."

"It's hawser-laid. It's the best three-strand that comes up this river, and it'll outwear any four that you could show me."

"I've no doubt of it. He asked for four." Brom let the end go. "A four's laid round a heart, and it keeps its round in a block, and a ferry's rope lives in blocks. You could give me this, and I'd still be owing him sixty yards of four." He put the purse inside his shirt, where the dead slips lay. "A man lent us a rope and told us what he wanted back. I'll not hand him something else and call it paid, however good it is. When you've four, there's a hundred and eighteen marks will come down this road to look at it."

The broker watched him go with his mouth a little open, like a man who has sold cordage all his life and has never before had any refused for being the wrong kind of excellent.

The border road left the river by a cart-track through alders, and at the first rise it turned its back on the south.

It was a road again as soon as the landing was out of hearing, and the hold came down on all of them as it had on every road in that country. The sigils stayed in their pockets. Cael's ten lay shut; the read asked at the first bend, civilly, what the wet ruts weighed, and was told that it need not know. Seln went ahead by thirty yards with his hands a little away from his sides. Lira came next, with the bow on her back. Brom had the mule's cheek-strap, with Karis at the off side of the load and Oryn at the near, and Cael walked behind the mule, as he always had.

Behind Cael there was a space of a dozen strides, and then a stick.

Nobody had told the man where to walk. A crew on a road has a place for each person in it, and each of those places had been found by somebody's getting hurt; nobody had yet been hurt for want of a seventh. So Vastin walked where a man walks who has been given no place, which is last, and a little too far back, and in the middle of the road.

Cael dropped back to him at the top of the rise. He had not looked forward to it. He was nineteen, and the man beside him had told people for forty years on which side of a line they stood; and Cael had now to tell him how to walk along a road.

"There's a rule out here, and you've been inside it since that landing. I'd sooner you had it from me than from the thing that it's about."

"I should be glad of it. I have noticed for twelve days that people on this road do not speak, and I took it for manners."

"It isn't manners. There's a thing that lives along the roads, grey, as high as a calf at the shoulder. The holds call it a stillhound. It'll walk beside you for a mile at forty yards, and you can talk, or drop a pot, and it only follows. What it's waiting for is a Path." He kept his voice at the pitch that the road allowed by day. "It hunts whatever goes out of a person when a Path's used. Karis has a word for it."

"Discharge," said Vastin. "It is on the form. An assessor marks it under presence, and I have signed for a great deal of it."

"Out here it gets you eaten. They come to it all together, from as far as they can feel it, and they don't leave off while it lasts. So nobody declares on a road. A carter who's Iron to the elbows will let his wheel go over a bank before he'll bring up the grey to hold it."

"And if one of them comes close?"

"Then you're a post. It goes by a silent person as it'd go by a tree. If it wants to be sure, it tries you with its teeth; and if you answer it, with anything, you've told it what you are. You give it something wrapped, and you stand. And since nobody speaks at such a time, there are hands."

He showed them as they walked. A flat hand, with the palm to the ground, was *stand*: wherever a person was, and as he was. A shut fist was *seen*, and one looked where the fist went and did not ask. Two fingers lifted were *come on*; that was Seln's, mostly. And a hand turned over empty, as a girl had once turned hers over to him on a hillside, was *nothing*: the thing had gone, or it had never been.

"Stand; seen; come; nothing." Vastin made each of them with his own right hand, in its linen, exactly, like a junior learning the seals. "I have them."

Thirty yards ahead, at a bend, a small grey figure lifted two fingers.

"Understood," said Vastin, in a clear carrying voice, to Seln's back.

The fingers stayed up a breath longer than they needed. Then they came down, and Seln went on round the bend without having turned his head.

"That's the one thing you don't do with them," said Cael.

"No. I heard it as I did it." The old man's face had not altered, but the back of his neck had gone a dull red above the frieze. "I have acknowledged every instruction that I was ever given, aloud, and I have required it of others. I beg your pardon. It will not be the last."

---

They made eleven miles that day, by Brom's reckoning, which was a mile more than a certain knee could do, and nobody remarked on it.

The halting-ground lay in a fold of the moor beside a beck, with a dozen rings of stones on it, and black in every ring. No fire had been lit there since the first frost. The carters of that country ate cold on a road, and Cael had long since left off missing a flame at dusk. He saw Vastin look at the rings, and ask nothing, having been told why.

They drilled him for the last of the light, and it was Lira who did most of it. She had taught a boy from another crew to wait for a pole, and she went at an Archmarshal in the same fashion and with no more ceremony, walking backward before him down forty yards of rutted road with her right hand going. He stood for the flat hand and came on for the two fingers; and when the fist was thrown out to the left he looked left, and found a thorn-bush, and regarded it with great attention.

"That's well enough," she said, low. "Now look at yourself. No, don't move. Look where you are."

He was in the middle of the road, upright between the two ruts, with his weight laid evenly on both feet and the stick grounded, as he had stood on a landing in Ostrand and at the head of a ferry's planks.

"Now look at them."

Brom had been walking behind him, with Karis and the mule. There was nobody behind him now. The mule was nose-down in the heather at the road's edge, with a large man bent beside her as if he were looking at a hoof, and Karis had sat down on a stone. Cael could not have found Seln at all, if he had not known where to look.

"You stood like a gatepost," said Lira. "It's the finest standing that I've ever seen. But a post is a thing that nobody looks at, and I looked at you first of everything on this moor. Do it by the ditch. Do it a bit bent."

"I do not know that I can do it bent."

"Then do it by the ditch."

He did it by the ditch, four times, until the light went. He was a little too far out each time, and each time a hair less so.

They ate on the stones of a dead ring, with the beck talking.

"May I ask a thing that I ought perhaps to have asked this morning?" Vastin had his bread in his hand, and he did not lower himself onto a stone until he had the question out. "You have told me what I am to do if one of them comes at me. You have not told me what I am to do if it comes at one of you."

"The same," said Cael. "You're a post. If it's got Brom by the arm, then Brom has given it the arm. He'll be bitten, and he'll not answer, and somebody with a rock will come up on its blind side while it's finding out what he is. It was done so at a ford."

Vastin looked at his bread.

"Then I had better tell you what I can promise, since I have never yet made an undertaking that I did not expect to keep. I can be silent, and I can walk at the ditch. I think that I can be bitten. I do not believe that I can stand a stride from a person who is being bitten, and do nothing." He spoke as low as the dusk required. "I have stood in front of people for forty years. It is the whole of what I was for. I have never stood beside one and watched."

Nobody at that ring made light of it, and Cael turned it over. A man who told you before a job where he would break was worth two who found it out in the middle; a one-armed man had taught him that, with a blank left in a book on purpose.

"Then you'll not be a stride from anybody but Brom. Whatever we find up there, that's your place: a stride behind him. He's the hardest of us to hurt, and the slowest to answer. If Brom's standing still, then it's right to stand still."

"I shall endeavour to. I cannot put it higher."

"I'd not have believed you if you had."

Lira had her chin on her knee. She had been studying the man across the dead ring with the look that she had once kept for names on a card.

"What are you? On a plate. You know what all of us are. You could have told Karis's rank from the size of her flame; I watched you not say it."

"It has not been asked of me since I took a desk." He gave the question the attention that he had given a mule's stool. "There is a Path on a roll, with a tier beside it, and I have not declared the one or been paid for the other in more years than you have lived. It does not go anywhere. It is for standing still. From what I have been told today, it is the worst thing that a man could carry onto this road, and I am very content to leave it where it is."

"That's not an answer."

"No. It is as much of one as I should care to see entered against a person who had been on your road for eight hours."

She let it go, and Cael saw that it had not displeased her.

With the dark, the quietest of the ten came up in him to the height of his knees, and he held it there, as he did at every dusk. A healer sat on the next stone with her sleeves down. She had looked at a stiff leg four times since the bread was cut, and Tide, the tenth, lay as still in him as she sat, and for the same reason: nobody had been asked.

Brom had brought seven bowls out of the pannier, and nobody had seen him count. He filled the seventh with what there was, which was cold barley and a heel of cheese, and set it on a stone; and Vastin thanked him by name. A little afterward a copper lay on that stone beside the empty bowl. Brom looked at it for a while, and left it where it was.

Cael wrote by feel, on his knee.

*Two hundred and seventy-eighth day. A halting-ground on the border road, eleven miles from the river. A slip taken at the plank: a clearance, the cut below the shoulder hold, twenty. Five hands. Pike has a sixth line on it for the healer, of her own road. Vastin walks behind us. He was not asked, and he was not told no. He has the four hands, and he knows where a post stands. He says that he cannot promise to watch one of us bitten, and I believe him. The rope on the far bank was three-strand; Brom would not have it. In hand, a hundred and eighteen.*

*I ruled on a job. I have still not touched the page.*

*Deployment since the Line: thirty.*

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-14.md

# Chapter 14 — Carters Declare

The copper was on its stone in the morning, with the frost on it, and Vastin stood over it for a moment before he took it up.

"That is the second coin which this road has declined of me," he said. "An old man with three carts left the first on a stump."

"It was in the pot," said Brom. He was strapping the pannier, left-handed, with his knee against the mule.

"So I was told at your board, and I paid there, and you let me."

"A board's got a keeper. That's his pot, and he'll have his copper for it from anybody who isn't on a sheet." Brom drew the strap home. "A road hasn't one. Out here it's whoever carried the barley, and I carried it. Put that away."

Vastin put it away. He did not thank Brom a second time, and Cael thought that he was learning that as well.

It was the two hundred and seventy-ninth day. They were on the road in the grey before the sun, which is dusk turned round, with a ground mist lying in the hollows of the moor as high as a man's knee. The country had risen all the day before. It was heather now, and bent, and outcrops of a dark stone that broke into plates, and the ruts climbed through it toward a long shoulder of hill that stood against the west like the back of a beast lying down.

Seln's right fist came up at the second mile.

It went out to the left and stayed there. Cael looked where it went, and for the space of several strides he saw only the mist and the heather; then a piece of the mist moved against the rest, and he had the shape of it. It was low, and long in the back, and it carried its head stretched out level. It was going along the slope at a walk, forty-five yards off, at exactly the pace of a mule, and not one sprig of heather stirred round its legs.

He had last seen one alive on a white floor, with its teeth in a girl's arm.

The fist opened and lay flat, with the palm to the ground.

Six people and a mule stopped where they were, and stood as they were. Cael had done it a hundred times, and it had never once been easy, since all ten of the things that he carried woke at the first grey and spoke together. Wind leaned his feet toward the next rise with a whole burst ready. Pressure began to gather under his breastbone, a breath at a time. The read asked to be let out for one pace and a half, to tell him what he knew already; and it did not feel like doing anything, and he shut it. He put them down, one after another, as a man goes round a house at night shutting doors.

Then he turned his head, slowly, by the width of a hand, to see what stood behind him.

Vastin was at the ditch.

He had gone to it without looking at his feet, at the flat of a hand thirty yards ahead of him. He stood in the wet bents at the road's edge with the stick's foot on turf, and he was not upright; he had let his shoulders come forward and his head hang a little, like a man who has stopped to get his breath on a hill. It was not well done. Anybody who knew him would have seen the effort in it. But there was nothing in that mist that knew him, and nothing in his two wrapped hands, which lay on the stick's head as quietly as they had lain on a trough.

The grey head swung, low, once to each side. It passed over a mule, and a small woman, and a large man bent at a girth. It passed over an old man at a ditch as it passed over the thorn beside him.

Seln lifted two fingers.

They went on, and it went with them. It kept its distance for the better part of half a mile, drifting up the slope where the ruts bent toward it and coming down where they bent away, and in all that time nobody on the road made a sound that a Path would have made. The mist thinned. A beck came down across the road in a spread of wet stones, and the mule's feet rattled in it. At the edge of those stones the grey thing stopped, as if it had come to a wall, and stood; and when Cael next turned his head there was only heather.

Lira put her right hand out at her side, and turned it over, empty.

Nobody spoke for another furlong. It was Vastin who did it first, and he had waited until the road let him.

"It did not look at me."

"No," said Cael.

"I do not say it as a complaint. I am trying to enter it correctly." The old man was a little out of breath, and he had kept his place, a dozen strides back, so that he had to pitch the words forward. "I have not gone into a room in forty years that did not turn to see who had come. I have just stood within fifty yards of a thing that hunts, and it went over me as it went over a bush. I find that I have no column for that."

"You were a post. A post is what it doesn't look at."

"Yes. I had understood the instruction. I had not understood that it would feel like being let off something."

He said no more, and nobody told him that he had done it well. Cael saw Lira turn her head a little, on the whole of her foot, and look at a ditch that was now a furlong behind them, and face the road again.

---

The road came up under the shoulder at noon, and there it divided.

The ruts went on to the right, round the foot of a spur of dark rock that came down off the hill like a buttress, and were lost between two banks of broken stone. A foot-track left them at a cairn and climbed straight up the spur's side, among sheep-scrapes, to a wall of the same dark stone with smoke going up behind it.

"That'll be the cut, where the wheels go," said Brom. "And that's the hold. I'd sooner be told what's in a thing before I walk the mule into it."

They took the track. It was steep, and the mule went up it as if she had been born on a stair, and Vastin went up it a step at a time with his mouth shut and the stick doing the work of the knee.

The gate had no axe-head on it, or anything else. It was a plain bar across a gap in a wall as thick as a cart, and the factor was at it before they had got their breath: a spare grey person in a leather apron, with a slate hung from the belt on a cord and a sheep-crook in one hand. A pair of pale eyes went along them and counted.

"Pike's five, for the cut. I asked for five, and I see five and two over."

"One's a healer, and she's on the slip," said Cael. "The other's walking behind us."

"I don't pay behind."

"He's not asked to be paid."

The factor regarded Vastin, who made the short bow, and it appeared to answer the purpose, since the bar was drawn. The yard within was small and very clean, with a long low house on one side and a salting-shed on the other. The shed's door stood open on a row of empty hooks.

"I've had no salt up since the first frost. I've had no wheel up of any kind. They're stood on your landing, I don't doubt, thirty of them, saying that they'll come when it's fit." The crook pointed down over the wall, to where the spur fell away. "That's my road. It goes through the rock there by a cut that my grandfather's people made, a hundred strides and a cart's width, and it's the only way a wheel can come to this gate. Since the frost it's had three carts off me. And a carter, and two mules."

"What took them?" said Karis. She had her notebook out.

"You know what took them, or Pike's sent me the wrong five."

"I should like it in your words. I have it only in mine."

The factor looked at her a moment, and then leaned the crook against the wall, as a person does who has been asked a question worth both hands.

"They all know what a road is. They come up from the river quiet as a burying, two days, and never a finger lit; I've had Iron men walk through that gate who'd let a beast die in the shafts on the moor sooner than bring up the grey. They know. Then they come into my cut." A hand went out flat, and tilted. "There's a seep at the head of it. It comes down the middle of the road, a stride wide, and from the first frost till the last it's glass from top to bottom, in a place the sun never finds. A wheel goes onto that. Then the cart begins to go, backward, down a hill, with a year's goods on her and a man's own two beasts in front. What does the man do?"

"He holds it," said Brom slowly.

"He's Iron, or he's Stone, or he's some small thing that he was given at fourteen and has kept in his pocket for two days. He's had it out before he's thought. Carters declare, on the ice." The factor spat over the wall. "And then they come up out of the sink."

"How many?" said Seln.

"There's a hole below the road on the north side, where that water goes into the hill. They lie up round it. I've seen five. I'd not swear to five, and nobody who's been nearer has come back to tell me better."

Cael heard that, and he saw that Seln had heard it too.

"They never came up for a cart that kept its feet," the factor went on. "Not once. A man could lead a mule through there at noon with her head held, and I've known it done all the cold term by them that had the nerve. It's the wheels. I can't salt a byre off a mule's back."

"And the terms?" said Cael.

"Twenty, on this bar, when I've led a beast of my own up that cut and down it again with my hand on her, and come home. Not when you tell me it's done." The crook was taken up. "There's a pot for a healer whether she's with a crew or not. There always was. And I'll thank the rest of you not to ask me what you're called."

"Nobody will," said Brom.

---

From the hold's back wall a sheep-walk ran out along the spur's crest, and at its end the ground stopped.

Cael lay on his stomach there with Lira on his left and Seln on his right, and looked down thirty feet of scree into the cut. He had learned in a library, from a list of other people's bouts, that a place is fought in before it is stood in, and he had never had a better seat to learn one from.

It was a hundred strides long, as they had been told, and it rose from his left hand to his right, from east to west, by perhaps the height of two men in its whole length. The road in the bottom of it was no wider than a cart and a man walking beside. On the near side, the south, the bank was this same scree that he lay at the head of, loose plates of the dark stone lying at the pitch of a steep roof. The north bank was low. A man could have stepped up onto it; and beyond it the ground fell away again, steeply, down more of the same broken stone, to a hollow.

"Fifty-five yards," said Seln, under his breath. "From the road's edge to the water. I have counted it three times against that thorn."

The hollow was round, like a bowl, and it lay in the spur's shadow, where the day never came. There was ice on it. The ice was grey and dull, with water standing on it at the edges, and Cael knew that grey: the shelf at Lowmarch had gone that colour a week before the river took it. At one side, under a lip of rock, there was a black place where it had not shut, and he could hear water going down there, a long way, into the hill.

Round that bowl's rim the stone had been scooped. There were hollows in it, each the length of a hound, worn as smooth as the seat of an old chair.

"Three," said Lira, with her lips. "No. Four. There's one with its head down."

"Five," said Seln. "Under the thorn."

They were grey on grey, and they did not move, and it took Cael the better part of a quarter-hour to be certain of the fifth. They lay as dogs lie that have nothing to wait for, each in its own scrape, with its nose toward the road.

"Five is what can be seen from here," said Seln. "There is a strip under the north bank that this lip hides, perhaps four yards of it, the whole length of the cut. I cannot tell you what lies in it. I should not care to say five to a factor who has just told me five."

"Then we'll say five that we've seen," said Cael, "and plan for more."

He went over the cut itself, a stride at a time, with his eyes. Down the middle of the road ran the thing that the factor had held out a flat hand for. It began at a wet place under the rock at the head, away to his right, and it came down the whole hundred strides as straight as a rule: a stride of blue-white ice, with the dark road on either side of it. In that shade it had never softened. He knew what such ice did to a weight. He had lain on a river with his face against two hands of it, and felt a cart come up out of a hole.

"Where do they come up?" he said.

"There." Lira did not point. "And there, and a third at the top. See the bank? It's worn. Three places, all in the upper half. The stone's a different colour where they've gone over it." She was silent for a little, studying the scree under her own chin. "This side's bad going. It's all plates. If I ran on that, it'd run with me. I could cross it slow, on my hands, or I could go down it sitting. I'd not care to be a thing with four legs trying to come up it in a hurry."

"Could you stand on it?"

"With two arms," said Lira. "I could now."

He let that go by, since she had not said it to be answered. He was looking at the foot of the cut, away to his left, where the banks ran out and the road came into the light.

There was a cart there, on its side, with one wheel in the air. Beyond it lay the bed of a second, without wheels, and a scatter of grey staves that had been barrels. A third had gone clean off the road at the bottom and lay in the heather with its pole snapped.

"All three," he said. "Right at the foot. Not one of them's in the cut."

"They slid," said Lira. "It's a hill, and it's glass. Whatever goes on that ice at the top is at the bottom before it stops."

"So that's where it was done. Whatever was done to them."

He put it with the rest, as a fact about carts. Seln had turned his head a very little at the words, as he did at a line in a paper that was not quite what it ought to be, and had said nothing, having nothing yet to say. Cael was to remember that afterward: he had lain on a rock for two hours with the whole of it under his eyes, and he had read it as far as the wheels and not a stride further.

---

Karis had her leaf of findings on the well's coping, with a stone on it, and the seven of them stood round it in the hold's yard.

"I shall read you five lines that you have heard, and then I shall say what I think they are worth here," she said. "It hunts what a Path gives off. It hears, and sees a little, and kills on neither. Water masks it. It has no leader, and where nothing moves first, it stands. And it learns." She laid her pencil on the last. "After three had been killed at a ford by people who had declared nothing, the others would not come near such people. I have that from one evening, at one river, and I shall not pretend that it is more. It is all that we have."

"Three," said Brom. "So we've to kill three, quiet, and the rest pack up."

"If they are like the others. I think that a hold will not pay us for an *if*."

"It's what there is," said Cael.

He had made the plan on the rock, and he gave it to them as he would have laid out a bout, from the ground upward. Brom had gathered a handful of pebbles, without being asked, and he set them down on the coping one at a time as each place was named.

"They don't come for a cart that keeps its feet. They come for a declaration. So we give them one: small, and short, and a long way from anybody. At the top of the cut, eighty strides up. One point of Ember on a twist of tow, on the ice. It's out in a breath. And whoever sets it doesn't stay there."

"They go to where it was," said Lira.

"They go to where it was, and it's gone, and nothing's moving. So they stand. That's her fourth line. Five hounds, or more, standing on a stride of glass at the top of a hill with their heads swinging, and us below them, as quiet as posts." He touched the pebbles. "Brom here, twenty strides down from the mark, on the south edge, where there's a flat stone to stand on that isn't ice. Lira above him on the scree. Seln over the north bank, where they'll not see him and I shan't. When they come down to try us, they try Brom first. He's wrapped."

"I'm always wrapped," said Brom. He had a meal-sack bound round his left forearm already, over the sleeve, with cord. "It's a boot for an arm."

"You've a bill."

"A bill wants swinging, and I've the one arm to swing it. A rock only wants lifting and letting go." He had chosen it on the way up: a plate of the dark stone as big as a loaf, with one edge like an axe. "I'll have the rock."

"And who is to be loud?" said Karis. "It is my fragment that you mean to use. I have set ten thousand such points, and I should not burn my hand."

"You'd be eighty strides up a hill of ice with nobody near you, and you've never had to walk away from a thing that was coming. I've done little else since my Kindling." He did not let it sound like a joke. "It's a piece of yours that I'll be using, and it's mine to pay for. I'm the only one of us who can be loud once and then be nothing. That's the use of me."

Nobody disputed it. He was more afraid than he had let them hear, since he had been tried by teeth, on a river bar, and knew to a grain what it was to stand still for it.

"Then here is the whole of the doctrine, for anybody who's not heard it in one piece," he said, and he did not look at the well's far side. "Nobody declares unless they're meant to be loud. One of us, once, in a place that we've chosen beforehand. Everybody else is dark from the foot of that cut to its head, whatever they see. If the loud one can't do it, then nobody's loud, and we walk out by the foot as we came in."

"A grain," said Karis. "Not a flame. You have a habit of letting it grow."

"A grain."

Lira had unslung the bow. She held it out to the healer, with its hook and its one bolt, and it was the left hand that she held it in.

"I can't span that lying on plates, and I'd want both hands for the stone. It's yours. I've only carried it."

"A season," said Oryn. "I'd noticed." She took it, and hung it on the mule.

"You'll have the foot of the cut," Cael said to Karis. "With the clock, and the mule, and the boxes. It's eighty strides from the mark and sixty from Brom, and it's the last place in that road that anything will come to."

"And I'm with the mule," said Oryn. She had her sleeves pushed up. "My linen's on her. I'll not be two hundred yards from my linen a second time."

That left one, and he had been standing at the well's far side with both hands on his stick.

"A stride behind Brom," said Cael. "On the edge, on the dry. If one comes down and tries him, you'll see it done from as near as you are to me, and you'll see him not answer."

"You know what I said to you at the ring."

"I do. It's why you're behind the one of us who can best bear it."

"I shall be a stride behind him," said Vastin. "I can undertake where I shall stand. I have told you what I cannot undertake, and I had rather say it twice than have you find it out."

"It's heard twice," said Cael.

---

They went into the cut from its foot an hour after noon, when the factor judged that the shadow lay deepest and the beasts below were most asleep.

Cael walked by the three carts. He had not meant to look at them, and he looked: a wheel with two spokes gone, and a barrel-head with a hold's mark burned in it, and under the nearest bed a mule's collar, with the straw coming out. Somebody had driven that cart for two days up a silent road with a Path in his pocket. He had kept it there until the very last hundred strides.

Karis stopped there, at the bank's end, with the mule's cheek-strap in one hand and Hesk's clock in the other. Oryn stood beside her with her arms bare to the elbow. Neither of them said anything, since there was nothing that the road allowed.

The cold came up out of the cut to meet him like the air from a cellar. He kept to the south edge, where a strip of dead turf and gravel ran beside the ice, a little wider than his two boots. The read asked at every stride what the stuff weighed, and Anchor asked *There?* of the wheel-ruts frozen into it, in its thin held note, as it asked at every sill; he told the one that he knew, and the other no. He did not look at the north bank, which was no higher there than his belt, or think of the strip under it that a lip of rock had hidden.

At sixty strides there was a flat stone at the road's edge, and Brom stepped onto it and set his feet, with the rock held against his thigh. Vastin stopped behind him. Cael did not see where Lira went; there was a small dry rattle on the scree above, and then nothing. Seln had left them at the foot, and was already nowhere.

Brom looked at him, once. Then the big man put his right hand flat, palm down, at his side.

Cael went on by himself.

It was twenty strides, and they took him longer than the sixty had. At the eightieth the cut's head stood above him, black and wet, with the seep coming out under it in a skin over the stones. He stooped, and laid a twist of tarred tow on the ice, in the middle, and stepped back two paces onto the turf. His mouth was dry.

He looked at the tow.

*Yes*, he told the fifth of the ten, for the first time in earnest since a stair.

A seed of yellow stood on the tar. It grew for one breath to the size of a barley grain, and that was all he gave it. He shut Ember as he would have shut a hand. The heat that ought to have gone somewhere came home along his left arm instead, though he had been holding nothing, and the old weal across that palm woke and stung as though he had laid it on a kettle.

He turned his back on the mark, and walked.

Wind had his feet before the first stride was down. It leaned him at the foot of the cut with everything that it had, and he told it no, and found that his own two legs had something to offer in its place: a walk, a little bent, close under the scree, such as a girl on a bench had beaten into him with the two halves of a count. He did not run. A thing that ran on that road would be looked at.

He heard them at the tenth stride. It was a sound like gravel poured from a cart, away up on his left, beyond the bank; and it did not come from three places. It came from the whole upper half of the cut together.

They came over the north bank at the mark, as Lira had said that they would, and he turned his head as far as a post may. There were five, grey and long, pouring over the worn places with their heads level. And behind those, out from under the strip that nobody had been able to see, there came three more.

He was two strides from Brom's stone. He stopped, and stood, and counted eight.

They reached the tow, where there was a smear of soot on the ice and nothing else. He waited for them to stand.

They did not so much as check. The first of them set its feet on the glass at the mark, and turned its head down the hill, toward the foot, where three carts lay; and all eight of them came on.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-15.md

# Chapter 15 — The Loudest Thing

He understood it in the time that it took them to cover the first five strides, and it was far too late to be of use.

They had learned the road. Nothing that had ever declared at the top of that glass had stayed there. It had gone down the hill backward, with a man hanging on its tailboard and a Path blazing in him, and it had come to rest at the foot; and three times the grey things had run to the head of the cut, and found the sound already sixty strides below them. They did not go any longer to where a declaration was made. They went to where it ended.

And at the foot today there were a small pale woman, and a healer, and a mule.

He had put them there himself. He had called it the last place in that road that anything would come to.

They came down the ice as he had never seen a beast go on ice. They did not gallop. They ran low, with their legs spread and their claws out; and where the runnel bent a hand's breadth they let their hindquarters swing wide, and caught themselves, as a boy does on a slide of his own making. They were as high as calves at the shoulder. There were eight of them, strung out over ten strides of glass, and the sound was like a handful of nails drawn down a slate.

He had come two strides below Brom's stone by then, and he stood there, as he had told six other people to stand. Brom was a post above him, with the rock against his thigh. Something on the scree had gone as still as the scree itself. That was the doctrine, and it was sound. They would go by. They would arrive at the foot, where nothing was loud, and they would stand there and swing their heads. Then one of them would try the nearest quiet body with its teeth.

Karis knew how to be a post. So did Oryn. He was not certain what a mule knew.

There was a brown coat at his elbow.

Vastin had not looked at the hounds since the first of them came over the bank. He was looking down the cut. Cael, who was two feet from him, saw exactly where. A mule's long ears stood against the light, sixty strides off, beside a small figure with something bright in its hand, and a square one with its arms bare. Then the grey head turned, and he looked up the hill at what was coming. His face did not change at all. It was the face with which he had read a case through to its last sheet.

"I beg your pardon," he said, quite low; and Cael knew afterward that it had been said to him.

He stepped out into the road.

It was one stride, bad leg first, off the dead turf and onto the frozen ruts at the runnel's edge. He set his two feet, a little apart, with the stiff leg straight. He took the ash stick in both his wrapped hands and brought its foot down on the road between his boots, once, as a man knocks at a door that he has the right to open.

There was a sound like a mallet on a cask. It did not come from the stick.

It came out of the road. Cael felt it through his own soles, since for that one instant the ground under him had been told something and had answered. From the place where the stick stood, a line went across the cut. He saw it run. It went over the frozen ruts and split the runnel's glass from side to side, as straight as a thing ruled, and struck the foot of the north bank. All along that line the ice was white, in a crack no wider than a hair.

Then the air stood up from it.

He had no other words for the thing, then or later. There was no light. Nothing was thrown, and nothing went anywhere. One moment there was a road going up a hill, and eight hounds on it. The next there was a face across the road from bank to bank, as tall as the scree. He could see it only as he might have seen a pane of clean glass, by what was on it. The frost that the pack had kicked up was drifting down the cut ahead of them. It came to that place and stopped, and lay flat on nothing.

It was two feet from his face. He could have put out his hand and touched it, and he did not.

The first hound was three strides off and running.

---

It struck with its whole weight, shoulder first, and the sound was that of a sack of wet meal thrown against a barn door.

The thing did not give by the breadth of a hair. It did not ring, or shake; the frost that lay on it never stirred. The hound went back up the hill on its side, with all four legs working, and the second went into it, and the third into the second. Behind those the rest were braking with everything that they had, sliding on their haunches down the glass. One after another they came against that face in a slither of grey bodies, and were stopped.

Eight of them had come at it running, and not one had got through. It held.

Cael had seen Paths used on floors for half his life. He had seen fire run between nine points, and a girl leave one place and arrive in another, and a Gold lay the air down in lanes; and every one of those had been a thing that went out and did something. This did nothing whatever. It was a plane, as flat as a rule and as thin as a thought. It had been drawn on the ground and stood on end. It kept the ground's own refusal to be walked through, and it would have been all one to it whether a moth struck it or a cart.

He looked at the man.

Vastin had not moved. That was the first cost, and Cael read it off a pair of boots. They stood on the frozen road exactly as they had been set, and they looked as though they had been let into it. The line that crossed the cut ran under both soles. Each time that a body struck the plane, forty feet off or four, something came down through that brown coat like a blow on a post. The stiff leg took it and shook.

"Can you walk it?" said Cael. "Forward. Can you push them up the hill with it?"

"No." It came through shut teeth, very distinct. "It does not go anywhere. I told that girl so. It stands where I stand, and I may not lift a foot; if I shift so much as a heel, the whole of it is gone."

"For how long?"

"I have never been asked to find that out."

That was the second cost, and it was arriving already. It was going out of him as heat goes out of a forge when the door is opened, steadily, with nothing kept back. Cael's own Arbiter had been dark since the day that it woke, and yet he felt it from two feet away. He had stood beside Brom when the grey came up, and beside Karis with nine points alight, and neither had been a tenth of this. Every hound in that cut could feel it from the far side of the hill. It was the loudest thing that had been done on that road in the memory of the den.

It did not slide away down the ice, either, and it did not stop. It stood.

They knew it in two breaths. He saw the grey heads leave the plane, one and then all, as a flock of birds turns. There was nothing in that hard clean face to bite. The thing that they had come for stood at its southern end, behind it, in a brown coat. There was no going through. There was a bank.

The first of them set its forefeet on the scree at the plane's end, a little below Brom's stone.

Brom dropped the rock on it. He did not swing. He had said that a rock only wanted lifting and letting go. He lifted the thing left-handed to the height of his chest, with no grey on him anywhere, and let it go edge downward. The hound lay down on the plates as a dog lies down by a hearth. It did not get up.

That was one, and it had been done without a sound that a Path would have made. Not a grey head turned to it. They went by a large still man as they would have gone by a boulder, onto the bank above him, seven of them, scrabbling.

Then the bank began to move.

"Yours," said Lira's voice, somewhere over Cael's head, not loud.

She was sitting on the scree, ten feet up, with her heels dug in. He had not seen her go there. She had a plate of the dark stone in her two hands, as broad as a trencher. She sent it down the slope flat, like a boy skimming a pond; and where it struck, the whole skin of the bank let go. A yard of scree went out from under two hounds together and carried them down to the road in a rattle of plates, on their sides, with their legs going.

She had the next stone up before they had found their feet. She lifted it with both arms. The left came a little after the right, as it did in everything, and the slope did not care which hand had been late.

"It runs," she said, between her teeth. "I told you. Nothing with four legs comes up this in a hurry."

But there were seven, and one bank, and it was thirty feet from the road to the lip. A thing that could not climb it quickly could still climb it. Cael saw one of them go wide of her, up to the left, where the plates were bigger and lay still. He saw another, which she had sent down to the road, come in under Brom's stone on the low side, and shut its jaws on the nearest thing that was quiet.

It was Brom's right boot.

The big man did not make a sound, and he did not answer. He stood on one foot and a hound, with a sack on his left arm that he had wrapped for this and had not been asked for. He looked down at the thing with an expression of deep and patient dislike.

---

The one that had gone wide came over the top of the bank's shoulder, above the place where the plane ended, and dropped onto the road inside it.

It was no more than a length from Cael. It did not so much as turn its eye to him; he was a quiet body, and there was a loud one two feet beyond. It went by his knee like a grey wind and took the old man by the stiff leg, a little below the joint, and shut its teeth.

Vastin made a sound in his chest, and that was all.

He did not lift the foot. Cael saw the boot stay where it had been set, on the line, with the whole weight of a beast as heavy as a calf dragging at the leg above it. The bandaged hands went white on the stick. From bank to bank the plane stood as flat and as still as it had stood before, with the frost lying on it. Beyond it a hound that the slide had carried down was running at the bank again.

Cael had his knife out. He did not remember drawing it.

Everything that he owned came to him then at once, and each of the ten had its reason. *Edge?* said the ninth, of the steel in his fist, and it would have gone through that grey neck like a wire through cheese. Pressure had been gathering since the first hound came over the bank. It lay under his breastbone as tight as a held breath, narrow and deep, and he had learned in a shed what it did afterward. It would go through the hound, and on, into a man's knee. *There?* asked Anchor, of the place where the plane met the scree, in its thin note. A point held there would have shut the bank like a gate, and it would have taken every other thing that he had while he held it. Storm showed him the air of the cut as a lane already half laid, from the head to the foot, and what a lane would do to six hounds; and after it he would be ten counts late at everything, in the one place where a beat mattered.

He could have used any of them. The road's rule was broken already, by a better man than himself, for a better reason than he had ever had. No hound in that cut would have turned its head for one point of Ember more.

That was what stopped him, and it stopped him as a hand stops a child at a door.

They would not have turned. He could have made himself as loud as he pleased, here, at the wall's end, and it would have altered nothing whatever. The sound that they wanted was here already, and he would only have been adding to it. Seven hounds were trying to get round a wall. Not one of the seven had any wish to go through it. Why should it? Everything that it had come for was standing at the end.

He saw the whole fault then, as he had once seen the fault in a bout from a library chair.

A wall was a fine thing if what the other side wanted lay behind its middle. This one had nothing behind it at all but sixty strides of empty road, and at the far end of that, too small and too quiet to be felt, the three whom it had been raised to cover. The only reason that the pack had for crossing that line was the man who held it up.

Somebody else would have to be the reason, and it could not be done from here.

"Mine," said Lira.

She had come down the bank. He never knew how; there was a rush of plates, and she was on the road beside him with the long knife in her right hand. She did not strike at once. She put out her left hand first, the thin one, which had been out of grey wool for fourteen days. She took the hound by the loose skin behind its skull.

It was slow. He watched the fingers close in their order, the first and then the next. But what they closed on did not get away from them. The hand that could not yet hit a thing held it, as it had held a reed and a stave and a carter's boot. With the head held, she had all the time that her right side needed.

It was done in one stroke, close in, with the whole of her quick shoulder behind it, and it was not a thing to look at. The jaws came open. The hound went down across the old man's boots.

"Two hands," said Lira. She was breathing hard, and she looked at the left with something very like astonishment. "It held."

"That's two," said Cael. "There are six."

Vastin had not looked down. There was blood, dark, soaking through the frieze below his knee, and his face had gone the colour of the ice; but his eyes were on the bank, where four grey shapes were coming over the plates again, more slowly, having learned where the ground was bad.

"I have it," he said. "I shall have it for some little while yet. I cannot tell you how long, and I cannot go to it or from it." He drew a breath through his nose. "If you have a use for a wall, I should be obliged if you would find it soon."

---

"I've a use for it," said Cael. "Don't let it down. Whatever comes at it."

He looked for a place, as a girl on a mounting block had taught him to look: not where he wanted to fight, but where he would as soon be a post for a count.

It was behind him, on the north side. The low bank ran down the cut there, inside the plane, with its top crusted in loose plates; and ten strides off, where the bank stood highest, there was a place on it as far from Vastin's end of the wall as the cut allowed. A straight line drawn from the south scree to that place went through the plane a little north of its middle.

He had never in his life spent a burst to get away from his friends. He had spent every one of them, since the Line, to get at something. Each time he had landed like a parcel, with a whole count for the thing to do as it liked with him.

*Away*, he thought. *Not at.*

He told Wind yes.

There was a crack like a wet sheet shaken out, and the cut jumped. He had left the road and come to the bank, with the ten strides taken out of the middle. The plates slid under his boots, and held. Then the lock came down on him.

He had known that it would. He stood on a low bank of scree in the shadow of a hill, with one arm half out for his balance, and for one whole count there was nothing in him that answered. He could not shift a foot or shut his fist on the knife. Even his head would not turn.

He did not need it to. He had landed facing them.

Every grey head on that south bank had come round. He saw it as a man sees a thing in a dream, slowly and all at once: four hounds on the plates, and two on the road beneath, one of which had that instant let go of a boot, and each of them with its muzzle swung to the place where a burst had ended. It was a new sound. It was sharp, and it was nearer than the old one by the width of a road, and there was no bank in front of it at all. There was only the road, going down.

There were six left. They came off the scree in one slide. The bank that Lira had loosened came with them, a grey river of plates, and it put them onto the runnel's glass already running, six of them, straight at him, down a hill.

He stood behind another man's wall and could not so much as lift a hand. He had trusted his feet and his skin to a great many things in his life: to a count, to a read of a pace and a half, to his own judgment of a floor. He had never before trusted them to something held up by another person's knee, and a person whom he had known as an officer.

The first of them reached the line.

The plane took it at the full stretch of its run, and this time there was no braking, since a beast cannot brake on ice with a hillside of stone coming down behind it. It struck, and dropped at the foot of that flat face, and did not stir. The others went in on top of it. They went in sideways and end over end, in a heap as high as his waist, with the plates rattling down upon them. A stride in front of his own boots, the frost on that clean face shook once and lay still.

"One," he said, aloud, to nobody, as a girl with her arm tied had once said it for him.

He could move.

The ice went in the same breath. The crack that the wall had made lay across the runnel like the cut of a knife. Under a heap of that weight, the glass on the uphill side of it broke up in plates of its own, and what was beneath came up black. There was water under it. The seep had been running there all the cold term, a stride wide and a hand deep, under its own roof.

Two of the heap were standing in that water when they found their feet.

He saw what it did to them. Water hid a hound from what it hunted, and hid what it hunted from the hound; a small exact voice had read it out at a ford. They stood to the hocks in the runnel with their heads lifted, and they swung them, slowly, to one side and to the other, like things that have been wakened in a strange room. The loudest thing on that road was twelve feet from them, and they had lost it.

A small grey man came over the north bank above the wall.

Cael had not known where Seln was until that moment. He stepped down into the heap as he would have stepped off a kerb, with a short knife held low. Nothing in it knew that he was there, since he gave off no more than a stone does. He bent to the nearest of the two in the water, with one hand on its neck as a man steadies a horse, and it folded at the knees. He did not look at it afterward. He had done it as he did a paper.

Brom had got down off his stone, with one foot dragging and a rock in his left hand. He lifted it over the other, and let go.

---

That was five.

Cael counted them from the bank's top, as he counted any room. There was one on the plates under Brom's stone. There was one across the road inside the wall, where a pair of boots had stood all this while with a hound lying over them. There was one at the foot of the plane, that had broken on it. Two lay in the black water.

Three had come out of the heap alive.

They had been last into it, and so uppermost. They got clear of the plates on the uphill side with their coats full of grit, and they stood for a breath on the frozen ruts with their heads low. Not one of them looked at the wall. Karis had it in her fifth line. Five of their kind had been killed in that place in less time than it takes to say so, and by nothing that made a sound. The only sound in the cut was a thing that could not be bitten.

The first of them turned to the north bank, and the other two went with it.

They went over the worn place and down, running, as they had come up. Cael, who stood on that same bank forty feet lower, saw the whole of it. Fifty-five yards of scree went by under them like water. At the bottom lay the bowl, round and grey in its shadow, with the scrapes about its rim. They did not go to the scrapes. They went straight out onto the ice, the three abreast, to get the breadth of it between themselves and the hill.

It had borne them all the cold term. He had known that grey for what it was two hours ago, from a lip of rock, and had thought nothing of it.

It let them through together, without any great noise. There was a long soft tearing, and a slab went down on one side and came up on the other, turning; and after that there was a piece of black water the shape of a slate, with a little grey ice rocking on it. He watched it for as long as he could have held his breath. Somewhere under the lip of rock the water went on going down into the hill, as it always had.

Nothing came up.

"Three," he said. "Into the sink." He found that he was not glad of it, and he did not try to be.

When he turned round, the wall was still standing.

It stood across an empty road, from bank to bank, with a dead hound at its foot and the frost lying flat on it. The man who held it was looking at him. He had not been able to see over the north bank, and he had not shifted a heel to try.

"That's all of them," said Cael. "Eight. You can let it go."

"You are certain."

"I counted."

Vastin shut his eyes. He did not do anything that Cael could see. The frost that had been lying on nothing fell, in a thin straight line, across the road and the broken ice. There was a hill with a cut through it, and cold air. Up on the scree a plate shifted and ran a yard, and stopped.

He did not fall. Cael had thought that he would, and was half way down the bank to him. But the man had both hands on the stick. He bent over it until his forehead was almost on his knuckles, and stayed so, with his feet where they had been. They had not moved since the stick came down. He lifted the left boot now, an inch, and set it down, as if to learn whether it was still his.

"I am perfectly well," he said, to the road. "I shall stand a moment. I should prefer not to be helped to sit."

"Nobody's helping you to do anything," said Brom. He had lowered himself onto the edge of his stone, which was a thing that he was allowed. He had his right foot out before him with the boot still on it. The upper was torn across from the lacing to the welt. "It had my boot. I said that I was wrapped."

"You wrapped the arm," said Lira.

"It didn't want the arm."

Cael stood on the road between them, with his knife in his hand and nothing on it. His left palm stung. He had spent one grain of fire and one burst, and neither had touched a hound.

He waited then for something else, and he did not know for a breath what he was waiting for.

Each of the ten had come to him so. He had stood within reach of somebody who was using a Path in earnest, in a fight that the person meant. Afterward, or in the middle of it, there had been a new thing in him, asking its question. He had just stood two feet from the largest declaration that he had ever felt. He had put his whole skin behind it.

Nothing came. He had ten, as he had had that morning. He could not have said why some arrived and some did not, and he did not try; he put it where he put a cord, and a hair.

Down at the foot of the cut a mule had begun to walk up the road, on the dry edge, with a small woman at her head. In front of them both, coming a good deal faster, there was a square figure with her sleeves pushed up.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-16.md

# Chapter 16 — A Month

Oryn came up the cut on the dry edge, at the pace that she kept for a gate with a bell ringing, and she stopped at her stride and a half and looked the whole of it over before she spoke.

It took her three breaths, in which Cael saw her eyes go to five grey shapes, and leave them, since they were past her trade. They went to a girl who was wiping a long knife on a tuft of bents with her left hand; to a small man who had come out of the broken ice wet to the shins and was looking at nothing; to a large one sitting on a stone with his foot out. They came to Cael, and passed over him as they passed over a gatepost. Then they stopped.

"That's first," she said.

Vastin had straightened. He had his back to the south bank and both hands on the stick, and below his right knee the brown frieze was black and wet from the joint to the boot.

"I am in no immediate—"

"I'll say when it's my turn to be told." She did not go nearer. "Three days ago, at a board, I opened my mouth about that leg, and you answered a thing that I'd not yet put. You said that you offered it to nobody. So I said that I still hadn't asked, and I let it lie, since that's my rule and I keep it." She pushed her left sleeve a little higher. "This is the asking. There are teeth in that, and under the teeth a joint that's been walked three hundred miles and then stood on by eight of those. I want my hands on it. And I want two fingers on your wrist while I'm there, to learn what's left in you. May I?"

He did not answer at once.

Cael watched him look for the form of it. A man who had spent his life granting leave, and refusing it, under his hand and a seal, was being asked a plain thing by a woman on a road who had no office to ask it from. He looked at her bare forearms. He looked, for an instant, at the five who stood about him, and it came to Cael that he was waiting to hear whether any of them would answer for him.

Nobody did.

"Yes," said Vastin.

It was the first thing that Cael had ever heard him grant to anybody without a clause hung on the end of it, and the healer would not have even that.

"To what? I'll not take a nod for it."

"To your hands upon the knee. And upon my wrist." He drew breath. "I said that I offered it to nobody. I find that being asked is another matter."

"It generally is. Sit on that stone."

"I had rather stand."

Brom, who was on the stone, had begun to get up from it. He lowered himself again, slowly, and looked at the healer.

"Then stand," said Oryn, "and you'll stand still, and you'll put your weight on the big one's shoulder and not on that stick. I'm not kneeling on a frozen road to have you fall on me."

He did as much of that as he could bear to, which was to lay two fingers on Brom's shoulder, as a man touches a banister that he does not mean to use.

She knelt, and slit the frieze with a small knife, from the knee to the boot's top, and laid it back; and she told him what she was going to do before she did it, as she told every person who had ever lain on her plank. There were four holes and a tear, none of them deep, since a stiff leg in a thick coat is a poor mouthful. Those she would close. It would take her a quarter of an hour, and it would feel like cold water poured into the bone. The joint was another matter. It had been wrung like a cloth, and she did not mean to spend on it.

"A thing that's torn, I can shut. A thing that's tired wants what I can't give it faster than it'll take it for itself. I'll bind that, and it'll have to do its own work."

"I understand the distinction."

"I'd be surprised if you didn't. Now hold your tongue."

Her thick fingers went round the leg, above the place and below it, and her eyes shut. Cael had lain under those hands. He knew what was passing along that shin now, slow and exact. Tide, the tenth of his own, lay in him as still as a pool, and did not so much as ask; it knew its owner's work when it was near. He had been handed the reading without the mending, a ruler and no saw. Watching her now, he could have said to a breath where along that shin her attention stood, and he could not have done one thing about what it found there.

When she had done, she was grey about the mouth. She had spent on four small holes what a child at some northern gate might want of her within the week, and she had weighed that before ever she knelt, as she weighed every spending; it was why the joint was to have linen and a month, and nothing more. She took linen from the mule's pannier without getting up, and two laths, and bound the knee from above the joint to below it, turn upon turn, until it would bend no more than a post. Then she reached up, and he gave her his wrist, and her lips moved to ten.

"You're at the floor," she said. "I've felt a well like that in men who'd held a roof up off their own children. There's nothing in you to draw on. If I'd tried to spend on that joint, you'd not have had the wherewithal to take it."

"How long?"

"A month."

She got to her feet by Brom's other shoulder.

"Thirty days in that binding, and on that stick. You'll not kneel on it. You'll not run. And you'll not stand another of those on it, for anybody, until I've had my hands on it again and said so."

"I shall be on the road tomorrow."

"I've no quarrel with roads. A road's walking."

"As to the last, I cannot undertake—"

"I didn't ask you to undertake it. I told you what it costs. Stand a wall on that leg inside the month, and you'll keep the stick for the rest of your days." She had turned to Brom already. "Boot."

"It's a boot," said Brom.
