"It was. Off. May I?"

"Aye."

The upper had been torn across from the lacing to the welt, and two of his toes had gone the colour of a plum. Nothing was broken. She bound them together, and told him that he would know about it for ten days and walk on it all the same; and then she lowered herself onto the stone at his side, with her head down, and her lips moved to fifty.

---

The factor came down from the lip while she was counting, leading a mule.

It was a hold's mule, old and sour, with a sack over its eyes, and the factor took it by the cheek-strap and walked it into the cut at the foot, between the dead carts, on the dry edge. Cael understood that he was watching the only test that such a person trusted. They came up the whole hundred strides, past six people and a healer, with a pair of pale eyes going to each grey shape in turn. They went as far as the black wet rock at the head. They turned there, and came down again.

"Five," said the factor, at Brom's stone. "I said five."

"There were eight," said Cael. "Three lay under your north bank, where nobody could have counted them. They went back over it, and down, and out onto the ice. It let them through."

The crook was leaned against the bank. The factor climbed the low side with the ease of a person who has done it since childhood, and stood on the top with both hands in the apron's belt, looking down fifty-five yards of scree.

"That's rotten. It's been rotten a week; I'd not have put a lamb on it." There was a long silence. "I never saw that water give back anything that went in."

"So it's clear," said Brom.

[[TIER-B: "clear because it's empty, not because it's safe" — the plan quotes this in part and does not pin its speaker; coordinator to supply the protected line and its tag here]]

Nobody added to it, and the factor came down off the bank.

"Empty's what I posted. I never asked anybody for safe. I've lived on this shoulder since I was born, and that seep'll have its glass back by morning." A hand went into the apron. "There's another den'll find it, in a year, or in five. They'll have to learn it from the beginning, as these did; and by then I'll have had thirty carts of salt up."

The twenty was counted out on the top bar of the gate, in four heaps of five, and a thumb was set to the foot of Pike's slip, where it left a mark like a crook. Cael had been paid at a good many gates by then, and he had learned to read a hold by how it paid. The factor at the axe-head had counted twice, like a man who has been cheated before; this one counted once, and did not look at the heaps afterward, like a person who has got exactly what was wanted and means to think no more about the price.

"A hundred and thirty-eight in hand," said Brom. He put the slip inside his shirt with the purse. "That's his, to draw the tack on. I'll have it after."

"I've a thing to ask, and it's not about the money." The factor had not gone in. The pale eyes were on Vastin, who stood by the gatepost on his stick with the leg bound stiff. "I was on the lip. I saw eight of them run into the air and stop. I've had Paths through this gate for forty cold terms, and I never saw that, and I'd know what to call it if a carter asks me."

Nobody answered for him, since it was his to say or to keep.

"It is called Iron Wall," said Vastin. "It is on a roll, with a rank beside it which would mean nothing on this road. I had not declared it in a great many years. I should be obliged if a carter were told that it was declared by a fool, in the wrong place, and that he was got out of it by five people who never made a sound."

"I'll tell them what I saw," said the factor. "It's all I ever tell anybody."

Karis had her notebook open on the gate's bar.

"I have read of that Path," she said, to the leaf. "There is a description of it in a manual of engagement fields, with a diagram, which I had always supposed to be drawn wrong. I saw it from the foot. It looked as though eight animals had run into the end of the world."

"What did you put?" said Lira.

"That a plane stood across a cut, from a line that cracked the ice. That it stood for as long as he did not move, and went when he let it. That eight struck it and none passed." Her pencil went on. "And I have added a sixth line to my five. *It goes to where a declaration ends, if it has learned that the declaring moves.* I had the three wrecks under my eyes from the foot for the better part of an hour, and I did not read them either."

"None of us did," said Cael.

"No. I enter it against all of us. I enter it against myself first."

---

The hold fed them in the long house at dusk, on mutton and barley, at a board by a fire that a man could have stood in.

There was no rule against a fire under a roof, behind a wall as thick as a cart. Cael sat with his back to it and felt the cold of the cut go out of him a little at a time. Shadow had come up to his knees with the dark, as it did at every dusk, and he held it there; the man whom it had come from sat two places down, and had not spoken since the ice.

Vastin had not sat at all. There was room on the bench, since Brom had left it, and he stood by the door-post instead, with the stick and the bound leg, and ate what was brought him from a bowl held in his hand, as a man eats at a muster. Lira watched him do it for some time.

"He's not sat since the cut," she said, under her breath.

"If he sits, he'll have to get up again," said Brom. "In front of us. With that. So he's not sitting."

Cael thought that it was very likely the whole of it, and he did not think the less of the man. It was Brom who spoke next, aloud, across the board, since he could not leave a figure unsaid.

"It held, though. Eight of them, running. I've seen walls that wouldn't."

"It held, and they went round it," said Lira.

"That is exact," said Vastin.

He had set his bowl on the sill. He spoke to the fire, and it was plain that he had been arranging the thing since the hound was lifted off his boots.

"I shall tell you what that Path is for, since you have seen what it is not for. It is a Path of the tribunal floor. It is taught for fields; and a field is ground where a thing has been declared, a hearing, or a square, or a gate with a crowd at it. Everybody on a field knows where the line is. They are there because they want what lies on the far side of it: a door, or a prisoner, or a paper under seal. I have stood on eleven such. Nobody on any of them ever went round me. There was nothing round me that they wanted."

"And today there was nothing behind you that they wanted," said Seln. It was the first thing that he had said. "Only yourself."

Cael had read of such floors in a library above Ostrand, in the lists of other people's hearings, and had never thought to ask what stood at the edge of them. It appeared that an officer had stood there, with a stick, for the whole of a working life; and he had been so sure of being the thing in the way that he had never had to learn what a wall was worth when nobody wished to pass it.

"Only myself," said Vastin. "I drew a line across a road and stood upon it. It did all that such a line can do, and it served no purpose whatever, until a boy went and stood behind the middle of it, where he could not lift a hand, and made himself worth the crossing."

"I'd seen it hold eight," said Cael.

"You had seen it hold once. I have known a good many officers who would not have put their skin behind a thing on that evidence."

He turned from the fire then, and looked at the board, one face after another, in the order that he kept.

"The wall held the line," Vastin said. "And someone else had to be the reason they wanted the line."

Nobody answered him. The fire ticked.

"I have never once in forty years needed someone else to be the reason."

[[TIER-B: "You do now," — the plan gives this line without its speaker; coordinator to supply the tag]]

Vastin received it as he had received the price of a loaf. He bowed his head by a little, toward the voice, and it was not the short stiff bow; it was a good deal less practised than that.

"I know fields. I don't know packs." Vastin took up the stick. "I'll learn or I won't."

He said it without any sourness, as a clerk enters a balance that he does not yet know how to settle. Then he asked the hold's woman, who was passing with a pitcher, where a guest might sleep who was on nobody's sheet, and paid her a copper for the answer, and went out by himself into the yard.

"He paid again," said Brom.

"He'll pay till somebody tells him that he needn't," said Lira. "And then he'll want to know by what rule."

Oryn had been standing by the hearth's end with her bowl. She watched the door until it had shut.

"He took every word of that," she said. "About the month. He heard it, and he told me that he couldn't undertake it. And he's not knelt since, or gone faster than a walk, or leaned on anything but that stick." Her spoon went round the bowl. "I've had patients who said *yes* to everything and did none of it. I never had one before who said *I cannot undertake*, and then did it to the letter, as if it were a thing that he'd thought of himself."

---

Cael wrote it in the byre loft, on his knee, by the light that came up between the boards from the hold's lantern.

The others were down already in the hay, where he could hear Brom turning his foot about to find a way that it would lie. He began as he began every entry, with the day, and with what could be counted.

*Two hundred and seventy-ninth day. The shoulder hold. The cut is clear of eight: five killed, by a rock twice, by a knife twice, and once by a thing that I shall come to; three through the ice of the sink. Twenty paid at the gate. In hand, a hundred and thirty-eight. Brom's right foot, two toes, ten days. Vastin's right knee: four holes shut by Oryn, the joint bound, on a stick for a month by her word.*

*I planned it wrong. They do not go to where a thing is declared. On that ice they go to where it stops. I put Karis and Oryn and the mule at the foot and called it the safe end.*

He stopped there, with the pencil's point on the leaf.

He had gone back over it a dozen times since the ice. Eight hounds would have come down to the foot of that cut, and found three quiet things, and stood. They would have tried one of the three with their teeth. He believed that it would have held; a healer and a researcher knew how to be posts, and a mule answered with nothing that a hound hunted. He believed it. He had not been asked to watch it tried, since a man with a stick had put a road's width of refusal in front of it, on his own judgment, with a knee that he knew would not carry him. The rule had been broken, and he had broken a rule himself, twice, for a calf and for a carter. He could not find it in him to enter the breaking as a fault.

He wrote the rest as it came.

*Vastin fought. He rooted the road and nearly got eaten for it — a wall's no good if you're the only thing making noise behind it. He knows fields. He doesn't know packs. He'll learn or he won't. Watched an Iron Wall go up two feet from me. Filed it. Note the day: an Archmarshal took a wound out here and there was no one to report it to. He looked almost free. Almost.*

He read that over; and the last of it he had seen in the yard, an hour ago, and had not known until he wrote it what he had seen.

The man had come out of the long house with his copper spent. He had stood under the lantern at the byre's door, looking down at the linen on his knee, and his right hand had gone to the breast of the brown coat, where a pen would have been. Cael knew the gesture by now. An officer hurt on duty wrote it up within the day, no doubt, under two hands, with the circumstances; there would be a sheet for it, and a tray. The hand had found a receipt in a small woman's writing, and nothing else. It had stayed there a moment. Then it had come away empty, and Vastin had looked up at the black shoulder of the hill, and for the space of three breaths his face had had nothing in it that Cael had ever seen there: no room that he was being fair to, and no file.

Then the knee had reminded him, and he had gone in.

*The point: Ember, one grain, at the mark. In earnest. The left palm, though I held nothing.*

*Wind: one burst, away, ten strides, to the north bank inside the wall. The lock was spent behind another man's wall. It cost me nothing but the count, and I was more afraid in that count than I have been since a hall.*

*Nothing came of standing by it. Ten.*

*Deployment since the Line: thirty-two.*

He ruled a line; and below him, in the hay, somebody had not gone to sleep either.

"You've written that it was your fault," said Lira.

"I've written that I planned it wrong."

"It's the same sentence in a clean shirt." The hay rustled. "I was on that lip as well. I looked at three carts for two hours and thought about my arm. Write that."

"It's not mine to write."

"Then write that I said so, and put my initial against it. She does it. I don't see why I shouldn't."

He wrote it, with an L., and shut the book.

---

On the two hundred and eightieth day they went up through the cut as soon as it was light, on the dry edge, in file.

The seep had been at work in the night, and where a heap of hounds had broken the glass there was a new skin already, clear as a window, with the crack still showing white beneath it in a line from bank to bank. The factor's people had dragged the five away, and nothing else in that place was any different. Cael walked past Brom's stone, and past the spot where a stick had come down, and did not put his boot on the line.

Vastin stopped there.

It was only for a breath, while he stood with the bound leg a little out and looked at a hair's breadth of white under new ice, as a man looks at his own name in a book where he had not expected to find it. Then he came on, a step at a time, with the stick doing what the knee could not.

"The mule's carried worse than you," said Oryn. She had the cheek-strap, and the three boxes sat on that brown back as they had sat since the landing. "Two of those would shift to the other side."

"She is carrying what she was asked to carry. I shall walk."

"I said a month on a stick. I never said that the stick had to go uphill."

"You said that a road was walking," said Vastin; and the healer shut her mouth, and Cael saw the corner of it go down, which was most of the way to a smile.

Beyond the cut's head the ruts came out onto the open shoulder, and the whole country changed. It was high moor, bare and brown, rolling away westward in long swells like a sea that had stopped. The wind came over it steadily from the west, with nothing in it but cold, and it had not left off since the first of them topped the rise. There was no tree. Far off to the north a line of blue hills stood up, with snow on them still. Somewhere under that western sky, not two days off, the country that kept no books came to its end; Cael had crossed that edge once, going the other way, by another road, with his name in every ledger behind him, and he had not turned his head to look at it then either.

Lira walked with her left hand out of her pocket. It was the fifteenth day, and she was carrying her own knife-belt in it, coiled, for no better reason than that it was something to carry.

They had gone perhaps four miles when Seln's two fingers went up, and then his whole hand, pointing off the road.

There was an outcrop of the dark plated stone a furlong to the south, as big as a barn, and under its eastern face the ground was green, where a spring came out. It rose in a basin that somebody had built round with stones, a long while ago, and ran over the basin's lip and away down the moor in a thread of rushes. Beside it a single slab of the rock had fallen flat from the face. It lay level, waist-high on its own rubble, twelve feet long and half as broad, with the outcrop standing between it and the wind.

Karis had stopped in the road.

She looked at that slab as Cael had seen her look at a wall of cut courses in a marsh. It was off the road by a furlong; a person might speak there. It was in daylight, and dry. And there was not a bench near it, or a meal-sack, or anybody's elbow.

"That is a table," she said.

"It's a rock," said Brom.

"It is the table that I have been looking for since the night before last. I said that I should count in daylight. I have carried six years of an office's paper for two days up a road and through a fight, and I have not yet turned a sheet of it." She had the flat out from under her arm already. "I want the forenoon. I want pebbles, a great many, about the size of a hen's egg, since there is a wind. And I want every person here who can read to wash his hands in that basin first."

"That knee wants the forenoon as well," said Oryn. "I'd have stopped you at the next water, whether there'd been a table or not."

Vastin opened his mouth.

"I am not telling you to sit," she said. "I know better by now. I'm telling you where the mule's stopping."

Cael looked at the road, which went on westward over the next swell and out of sight. Somewhere beyond that swell there was a fork, and beyond the fork a rise. He had ruled on a job, and the job was done and paid, and nobody had asked him this morning which way they were walking. They had simply walked.

He went and washed his hands.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-17.md

# Chapter 17 — Three Readers

Karis laid the six rows out along the slab in the order that they had lain in the leather, with the newest at her left hand, and she put a pebble on every corner of every heap before she let go of it.

The wind could not reach them there. It went over the top of the outcrop with a steady hollow noise, like breath across the mouth of a jar, and now and then a little of it came round the rock's end and lifted a sheet's edge by the width of a finger. The sun stood a hand above the eastern moor. It was a thin white light, with no warmth in it, and it showed every ruled line.

"I shall count first," she said. "Nobody is to read me anything until I have counted."

She slipped each tape in turn, and went through the row beneath it by the corners, with her gloved finger and her lips; and she did it twice. She wrote each figure in her notebook under the row's place, and said none of them aloud. It took her the better part of an hour. Nobody hurried her. Lira brought pebbles from the basin's overflow in the lap of her coat, a great many more than were wanted, and set them along the slab's edge in a row, by size.

Cael had washed his hands with the rest, and he stood at the slab's far side, where every sheet lay the wrong way up to him, as paper always had.

He had not known where else to stand. He had touched neither the cord nor either strip of brass for a hundred and twelve days, by his own ruling, since a thing that measured had read one way in four hands and another in his. Paper was not a cord. A sheet said what it said, whoever turned it. He told himself so, and kept his hands on the rock's edge.

Oryn would not come near the slab at all.

She had unloaded the three boxes herself and turned the mule out on the green. She had looked once at six heaps of an office's paper weighted down with river stones, and her mouth had gone as it went at the smell of a wound that had been left too long. Then she had taken her strip of canvas to the basin, and a cake of grey soap, and begun to wash linen with her back to all of them.

"That's your trade," she said, when Lira asked. "I'll hear what it comes to. I'll not stand and watch it done."

Brom sat on the basin's rim with his right boot off and his bound toes in the overflow, which was as cold as a knife. He had been told by nobody to do it, and the healer had looked at the foot, and said that it was sense, and gone on wringing.

"Now," said Karis.

She had gone back to the uppermost row. She went through it a third time, quickly, looking only at the heads, and she laid a finger of her left glove on the rock for every group of four signs that she had not yet met in that row. The thumb went down last. She did the same with the second row, and the third, and so to the sixth, and each time the thumb went down, and no sixth finger was wanted.

"Five," she said. "I said more than four, by a lamp, and that I should give a number when I had checked it in daylight. It is five. It is the same five in every one of six years; I cannot read a sign of them, and I can tell one group from another as I can tell your five faces."

"Four on the hide," said Brom. "And one over."

"Wait. I do not yet know that our four are among them. I have only supposed it." She had the calfskin out, and she unrolled it at the slab's end, under four pebbles of her own choosing, with the pencil line running through its four rings. "The Stair I know, since his book gives me its signs. For the others I have only the measures. Three of our places were of one size, and the fourth was twice that, by the bend of its fence. If these sheets say the same, I shall believe them."

She took one sheet from each of four groups, and laid them side by side, and read down the third column of each with her lips moving. A cord knotted at the fives had been run out from a centre to an edge. She did not need to turn lengths and knots into anything; she needed only to see whether four entries agreed.

"Three alike, to the stride," she said. "And one that is double. They are ours."

"And the fifth?" said Lira.

Karis looked at the fifth group's sheet, which she had not lifted from its row.

"The fifth is larger again than the fourth. I have read its measure and nothing else, and I shall say nothing else until I have turned every sheet that it has. It is not on the hide. I do not know where it is. These are an office's signs for a place, and I cannot read them; if I could, I should not have needed to walk here."

Nobody spoke. Cael looked at the calfskin, where a ruled line went through four rings and off the edge of the skin onto bare rock. He had watched a small pale woman slide a strip of brass along that line, a hand at a time, on the first night of a cold term, past the fourth ring and onto the table, as though the deal might have something to say about where it went next.

---

"Then I shall read for what repeats," said Karis. "It is the only reading that I am good for, with a text whose words are shut. Seln, you will read for what is not there; you have told me a dozen times that it is the half of any paper. And you—" she had turned to the man who stood at the slab's end on his stick, with his bound leg a little out— "will tell me what the form is. I do not know this office. I do not know what a clerk in it is obliged to write, and so I cannot tell what he has chosen to."

"I can tell you that," said Vastin. "It is very nearly the only thing that I am still able to do."

He had not sat. Oryn had rebound the knee at the basin as soon as they halted, with him standing against the rock, and had said nothing to him about stones.

Karis put one gloved finger at the head of the nearest sheet.

"There are five columns, and they are the same five on every sheet in six rows. The first is the day, in the office's reckoning. The second is the station: he measures from eight points round the edge, by the compass and its halves, as the man did whose book I have. The third is the measure. The fourth is headed *Instruments*. And the fifth is the narrow one, which is headed in plain letters for the anomaly."

She went down them, a column at a time, across all six rows, and it took her a long while, and she gave it to them as she found it.

The measure did not change. That was the first thing, and she read it three times before she would say it. Each of the five places had been run out from its eight stations in every one of six years, by four different surveyors, to judge by the figures' shapes; and not one radius had altered by a single stride. She had expected that a thing would grow, or shrink. A marsh did, and a river-bed. These had sat where they were as a millstone sits.

"Whatever they are, they are not spreading," she said. "And they are not going away. I had not known until this morning that I was afraid of the one or hoping for the other."

The fifth column she had read by a lamp already. There were three clerks' hands in six years: a round one, and a cramped one that leaned backward, and one that made its capitals like gates. Each of them had written the same single word against every station of every place.

"The fourth is the one that I had not looked at," said Karis.

She was silent over it for some time.

"He is required to say how his instruments behaved. I suppose that somebody once wondered whether a cord, or a level, would go wrong in such a place as a sigil does. So there is a column. And in it, against every station, for six years, in four hands, there is one entry." She read it out. "*Cord true. Edge true. Level true.*"

Cael felt it in his stomach before he had understood why.

Nobody looked at him. He was grateful for that, and he knew what it cost at least one of them. A surveyor's cord had been carried into each of those places, year upon year, by men whose business was to notice if it read amiss; and it had read true, every time, as it had in an inn yard for Karis and Lira and Brom and Seln. There had never been anything the matter with the cord.

Karis did not lift her eyes from the sheet. She had a hair pressed in the fold of her notebook, and a line under it that said *no inference entered*, and she turned the leaf of the abstract over a little faster than she had turned the others.

"There is a sixth thing on the older forms," she said. "It is not a column. It is a space at the foot, ruled off, and headed *Remarks*. It is empty on every sheet in the three lowest rows but one."

She drew that one out. It was from the fourth row, and it had the Stair's signs at its head, and the number thirty-two. At its foot, in the ruled space, there were four words in a hand that was small, and upright, and pressed hard.

She did not read them at once. She reached behind her, to the tarred case, and brought out the dead man's ledger, and laid it open beside the sheet; and she looked from the one to the other for as long as it took Brom to draw his foot out of the water.

"It is his," she said. "I should not swear to a hand on four words. I shall say that I see no difference." She read it then, to the whole slab, in the voice that she kept for another person's finding.

*Behaves as device. Cannot be.*

"What does?" said Brom.

"The place. He has written it against the fifth column, under the word that they all write." Her finger lay beside the ruled space, not on it. "He was a man who lived among instruments. He knew how a made thing behaves: it does the same at every hour, for every person who comes to it, and it has an edge that you could rule. That is what he found at the line. And then he has written that it cannot be one."

"Why can't it?"

"He does not say. I suppose that nobody could tell him who had made such a thing, or how it would be made; and a surveyor is not paid to suppose." She had taken her hand away. "I felt it, at three of those lines. So did four of you. There was no dimming. It went out at one stride, for a third rank and a first and a Copper alike, and came back at the same stride, whole. It was not as a person tires. It was as a row of lamps—"

She stopped.

Cael had never seen her stop so. Her mouth stayed a little open on the next word, and he watched her look at it, as she would have looked at a figure that had come out too neatly.

"No," said Karis. "I have a rule for this, and I said it twice in a yard. I was about to tell you what a thing is from how it felt in my own hand, and my hand is the only ruler that I had there. I shall not finish that."

"You'd got as far as lamps," said Lira.

"I had got a good deal too far."

---

Seln had read all six rows while she was at the fourth column, standing at her shoulder and then at the next heap, and he had not touched a pebble that he did not put back.

"I have five things that are not there," he said. "I shall give them in the order of their weight, which is the reverse of the order in which a person notices them."

He had his hands in his sleeves. He spoke to the paper.

"There is no routing head on any sheet. That you knew. There is no surveyor's name; a field book is signed, and an abstract ought to carry the name that the book carried, and these carry a number in its place. There is nothing to say who ordered a man to walk round the same five places for six years. And there is no column for a cause." He let that stand a moment. "A form is a list of the questions that its maker expected to be asked. Somebody expected to be asked the day, and the measure, and whether the cord was true. Nobody expected to be asked why the thing happens. I have never before seen an office measure a matter so carefully and show so little wish to understand it."

"That is four," said Karis.

"The fifth is yours. You found the remark; I looked for what came after it." He drew a sheet from the third row, and one from the second, and laid them beside the Stair's. "On the form that he wrote upon, there is a space at the foot for remarks. On the next year's printing there is none. The rule that closed it off has been taken out of the block, and the fifth column runs to the foot of the sheet. Somebody read those four words. It is the only sign in six rows that anybody read anything at all; and what he did, having read them, was to see that no surveyor should have room to write four more."

Lira let her breath go through her teeth.

"And the man who wrote them went down forty steps with a stake," said Brom slowly, "and never came up."

"I do not join those," said Seln. "I only lay them where they can be seen."

Vastin had listened to the whole of it with his head a little bent, as he might have listened to a junior presenting a file. When he spoke it was to Karis, since it was she who had asked him.

"You wished to know what the form is. It is a survey abstract of the second schedule: that is to say, it is the sheet that stands in place of the field book when the book has been called in. The book itself does not survive it. I think that you hold the only one that ever escaped; and it escaped because its owner did not come home." His left hand, which would not open, lay on the stick's head. "The sheets are drawn at the year's end, in the office that has the charge of the stations. You will have seen that the days run strangely."

"I have seen that I cannot make them agree with the broker's paper, which gives a day past the Line."

"You will not. That office does not keep the seat's year; it keeps its own, which opens later. It is an old quarrel about who was founded first, and I shall not weary you with it. A day that is written in the stations' count must be turned before it can be laid beside a registry date, and the turning is a matter of taking off the difference." He gave the difference, which was a number of days that any child could have subtracted, and Karis wrote it in her margin. "Every junior does that sum in his first week, and gets it wrong once. A return that comes up from a station is dated so. A paper that goes down from the seat, to a district or to a person, is dated the other way."

"Thank you," said Karis. "That is the first thing in these rows that anybody has been able to explain to me."

He had been looking along the slab while he spoke: at six loose heaps, and their tapes lying in a tangle, and the wind lifting a corner.

"It should be directed that each row be taken up singly, and re-taped before the next is opened," he said, in a different voice, level and a little quick. "Under two hands. A schedule to be made of every sheet as it is turned, with its row and its place in the row, and the schedule to be signed by—"

He stopped. Nobody had interrupted him. Karis had her pencil lifted, and was regarding him over it with her head on one side; and Seln, at the next heap, had turned his eyes from the paper to the man, and kept them there, and said nothing whatever.

"I beg your pardon. I was drafting."

"It is quite a good order," said Karis. "I have done the first half of it already. I have nobody to sign the second."

"No." He looked at his own hand on the stick. "No. Of course."

It was not a quarter of an hour before he did the other thing. She had laid the Stair's sheet back, with the four words at its foot, and he had bent to read them for himself; and Cael, who was watching, saw the grey head come up very sharply.

"That is restricted matter. A finding of that kind, upon an instrument of that kind, would stand under the first article of the closed schedule, and no person below a seat ought to have sight of it without—" The quick level voice ran on for three words more, and died. "There is no schedule here. I find that I had classed it before I had finished reading it."

"What class were we?" said Lira.

"The class of persons to whom it may not be shown."

"That's been most of my life," said Lira. "It's a comfort to hear it from the man who kept the list."

He took that without a word. Seln's eyes had not left him.

---

"There is a thing at the head of each sheet which I have left until last," said Karis, "since I knew that I should not be able to keep my temper with it."

She had the four sheets side by side again, the three that were alike and the one that was double. She laid her pencil along the top of them. To the right of the four signs and the number, on every one, a small square had been ruled off by the printer, with a heading above it in the same signs that nobody could read; and in the square, in ink, a clerk had made a mark.

It was a short stroke, and beneath the stroke a bar.

Cael knew it before she had moved the pencil. It was struck into the head of an iron stake that lay in a tarred case, three feet from her elbow, with its point still bright. It was on every bar of a fence that he had counted in the dusk. A dead man had set it under four signs in his own book, where nothing obliged him to.

"I found that on two of our places, in the field, and not on the others," said Karis. "I have turned it over for a hundred and sixty-five days. I could make nothing of two with it and two without. And here it is on all four." Her pencil touched each square in turn. "The Fallow has it. The hall has it. A man with a punch did not trouble to strike it on their iron, I suppose, or struck it on a bar that we never dug for. The office has never once left it out."

"So it's nothing to do with which got fenced," said Brom.

"No. And it is nothing to do with size." She laid her finger on the sheet whose measure was double. "This place is twice the others, and it has the same stroke and the same bar as they have, drawn no larger and no differently. Whatever that square is for, it is not for how large a thing is."

"Then what's it for?" said Lira.

"It has a square. A printer cut a block with a ruled place in it, and a heading over the place, for a clerk to fill. Nobody rules a place on a form for a thing that is always the same." She was speaking very fast now, and very small. "It is a class. It is the kind of thing that a certificate has, at its head, where it says what its bearer is. And I shall tell you one thing more, and then I shall stop, since I can see the edge of what I know. On the sheets of the fifth place, that square is not filled as ours are."

"How is it filled?"

"I shall draw it, exactly, and show it to nobody until I can say what I am showing. I have learned what comes of describing a mark before I can read it. I had two with and two without for the whole of a cold term, and I made nothing of them."

She took the dead man's ledger, and opened it at its first page, and set it down above the four sheets, so that a small pressed stroke and bar in pencil lay over four in a clerk's ink.

[[TIER-B: "Two copies of one ignorance." — the plan gives this line without its speaker; coordinator to supply the tag here]]

Nobody at the slab asked who had filled that square, or by what rule, since there was not a person there who could have answered. Cael looked at five small marks. He had been stood on a plate once, and something had been written at the head of a sheet about him, in a place that a printer had ruled for it. It appeared that a hole in the ground could be filed in the same fashion. He put that where he put the rest, and added no verb to it.

Vastin had drawn breath, and Cael saw him shut his mouth on it. It was the first time that he had done so of himself.

---

It was a little after noon when she began to tie the tapes.

"One thing more, before these are shut," said Karis. "I have a figure for every row, and I made each of them twice; but I made them both, and a count that one person has made is only a hope. I want a second hand on each. Take a row. Count its sheets, by the corners, and say nothing until you have the figure. Then I shall tell you mine."

She gave the first row to Lira, who read everything with her lips and would count so. The third went to Brom, who dried his hands on his breeches a second time before he would touch it. Seln had gone to the basin, and stood there looking at the water with his back to the slab; she set the fourth by for him, and took the sixth herself, since it was the oldest and the softest.

The second she slid across the rock to Cael.

He looked at it. It lay in its loosened tape, a heap of printed sheets as thick as two fingers, and it was the first thing that anybody had put into his hands to be measured since a cord in a yard. He had been waiting all morning to be passed over. He saw now that she had not passed him over, and that she had done it on purpose: a small pale woman had thought about whether to give him a row to count, and had given it.

"By the corners," she said, without looking up. "They are thin."

He counted them. It was not hard. He lifted each sheet by its lower corner with one finger, and let it fall upon the last, and said the number in his head; the stock was soft, and it clung a little, and twice he went back three sheets to be sure that he had not lifted two together. He came to the row's foot, and to a figure.

He did not trust it. He turned the whole heap over, face down, and counted it again from the back.

"Lira," said Karis.

Lira gave her figure.

"That is mine. Brom."

Brom gave his, and it was hers.

"Cael."

He told her what he had. He saw her pencil go to the place in the notebook, and stop there.

"Again," she said, quite gently.

"I've done it twice. From the front and from the back." His mouth had gone dry. "It's the same."

"Then you are one short of me."

The wind came round the end of the rock and lifted the corner of the uppermost sheet, and let it down. Nobody at the slab moved. Lira had turned her head; Brom had his thick fingers flat on the third row, as if it might be the next to go.

Cael looked at his own two hands, which lay on either side of a heap of paper. It was a cord that he was thinking of, run out three times across an inn yard, with a slip of ash laid down at its end and a scratch on a stone trough under the middle of the slip. It was a hair, in a gap that had been measured for it. He had been one short then. He had held the thing exactly as four other people held it, and it had come out a piece under what it was.

He had thought that paper would be different, since a sheet either lay in a heap or did not.

"Count it for me," he said. "With your own hands. I'll not touch it."

Karis had begun to rise from her stone. She had the look that he had seen on her in a store behind a chimney, with her cheek almost on the deal, and it was not unkind; it was a great deal worse than that. It was careful.

A sleeve came past his shoulder.

He had not heard anybody cross the turf. A small dry hand laid a single sheet down on the rock in front of him, between his two hands, and squared it with one finger, and went away again. It was thin stock, and newer than the rest, and it had been folded once. It had a printed head. At its foot a bar of black lay across the paper, as broad as a thumb, cracked like mud in a rut.

It had not been laid at the foot of the second row, where it belonged, and it had not been given to Karis, who was the keeper of it. It had been set before him. And the man who had set it there did not say one word.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-18.md

# Chapter 18 — A Year and a Day

Seln had taken the sheet an hour before, from the foot of the second row, while a small pale woman was reading a dead man's four words aloud; and it had troubled him a little that it was still so easy.

He had lifted papers out of heaps under their owners' eyes for the better part of his working life. One did not snatch. One was seen to be handling the heap for an honest reason, as he had been, reading six rows for what they left out. One's left hand squared the sheets while the right went on turning them. The sheet was in his sleeve before the pebble was back on its corner. Nobody at the slab had looked up. They were the only six people living from whom he had never before taken anything.

He stood at the basin now, with his back to the rock, and held the thing low against his body, where the wind could not get at it.

He had read it once already, on the night of the two hundred and seventy-seventh, over a shoulder, by one lamp. He had not meant to read the date at all. Karis's pencil had come to it, and written a year and a day in a clerk's figures, exactly as they stood; and a man who has copied the seat's paper for fifteen years does not see a station's date without turning it, any more than a carter sees a load without knowing what it weighs. The turning had been done before he knew that he was doing it. A day had come out.

He had known that day. He had written it.

It stood at the head of a file. It was the first line of the first sheet, after a name and before a word in square brackets, where the registry sets down the day on which a person was classed. The file had lain open on a good many desks at a house above Ostrand, in the years when he was employed there to carry such things from one desk to another, and to be overlooked. He had copied that line twice with his own hand, for two different officers. A clerk forgets a face in a week. He does not forget a line that he has copied twice.

So he had stopped. He remembered it without any pride: his hands, and his breath, and the eyes that he had not been quick enough to take off the pencil. The boy had seen. He saw most things, though he was slow to say so. Seln had said that he had read it, which was true, and had gone to a window and looked at nothing for the rest of a lamp.

He had not been certain. That was the reason that he had given himself, and it had been a good one for two days.

A sum done in the head, by lamplight, over another person's shoulder, at the end of such a day, by a man who would have paid a year's wages for it to come out otherwise, was not a finding. He might have taken one figure for another. He might have had the difference wrong; it was a long while since he had needed it. He might, after fifteen years of files, have laid the wrong day at the head of the right one. There were three places where he could have slipped, and he had spent two days on a road going over each of the three.

This morning a man on a stick had given the difference aloud, to a notebook, in front of seven people, and it was the difference that Seln had used.

He did the sum again now, properly.

He did it first as he had been taught to, taking off. Then he did it the other way about, from the day that he remembered, adding, to see whether he came back to the figure on the sheet. He checked the year. He turned the sheet over, and looked at the brush's work on its back, and turned it again. He read the technician's date a figure at a time, with his thumb moving under it, as he had not read anything since he was a junior. He tried it a third time by a different road, through the day column of the abstract that had lain next to it in the row.

It came out as it had by the lamp. It came out so all three times.

He stood for a while and watched the water run over the basin's lip.

He did not ask himself what it meant. He had a rule for that. It was older than the one that Karis kept, and a good deal less handsome. A clerk who guesses at a file in front of its subject has written the subject's finding for him. He had seen it done to this boy since his Kindling, by men who were better paid than himself and no wiser, each of whom had looked at an empty column and found it unbearable, and had put a word in it. He did not know what a sequence was. He could not have said what it was for one to be initiated, or what lay under a plate, and neither could the man who had carried the sheet three hundred miles. He knew that two papers agreed about a day. He had no more than that, and he did not intend to have more.

There remained the question of what a person did with it.

---

His training allowed three things to be done with a fact, and he went through them in their order, since it was the only order that he had.

A fact could be filed. One kept it, and said nothing, and let it lie against the day when it should be useful; and he had kept a great many so. It was what a desk did. He had cut a hundred and forty-seven notches in two hazel wands to count what a desk would do with a clerk's packet, and had been wrong about the manner of it; but he had not been wrong about desks. There was an office somewhere that had held six years of sheets where nobody would receive them. He had been reading its work all morning. He did not care to find, at his age, that he had become a small copy of it, with a locked case for a drawer.

He did not look down at the strap across his chest. He had carried that case for twelve years, shut, and it was shut now. He had found before today that he could not hold two closed things up to the light in one morning. He made no ruling upon it.

A fact could be reported. That was the second way, and it was the one that an honest man would be expected to take. He had only to walk back to the rock and say eleven words.

He considered it with care, since he distrusted his own distaste. If he said it at the slab, seven people would have it in the same breath. Karis would be obliged to enter it; she entered everything. She would then be obliged to refuse the sentence that followed from it, aloud, with the boy standing there, and he had watched what that refusal cost her once already today. A man who had certified readings for forty years would hear a day named, and his mouth would open on an order before he could prevent it. A healer would hear it from the basin. And the boy would be told one more thing about himself, in public, by a clerk.

He could take the boy aside. That was better. But it would still be Seln's mouth, and Seln's choice of words, and Seln's face to be read while he chose them. Afterward the boy would have to decide what to tell the others with somebody standing by who already knew. He had been handled so since he was a child. People had found things out about him and then come to tell him what they had found.

There was the third way. Seln had used it oftener than the other two together, and never once on a friend.

One placed a thing. One did not say it. A paper was left where the right person would come upon it, at a time when he could read it unobserved. He was let to make of it what he would, and was never afterward asked. It was how a man was turned, in the old trade, or warned off, or ruined. It was done so that the subject should believe that the knowledge was his own. Seln had been excellent at it.

He looked at that squarely, standing over a spring on a moor with a sheet in his sleeve.

The kindest thing that he knew how to do for the boy was, in every particular that could be seen from outside, the same act as the cruellest thing that he had been trained to do to a stranger. He could find no way round it. The difference lay wholly in what he wanted. He had been taught, by people who knew their business, that what a man wanted could not be seen and ought never to be trusted.

He wanted the boy to have it, and to be free of him in the having.

That was all. The boy could read a registry date; he had read them upside down on other people's desks for as long as Seln had known him. He had heard the difference given an hour ago, and he forgot nothing that had a number in it. If the sheet lay before him he would turn it, or he would not. If he did not, it was not yet his to carry. If he did, then he might write it or burn it, and tell five people or none. Nobody alive would have the right to ask him what he had made of it. Seln would least of all, having said nothing.

It had a cost, and he made himself price it, as that large young man would have.

A thing given so cannot be shared. He would never know for certain that it had been read. He could not stand beside the boy in it, or be asked what he thought, or say that he was sorry. He could not be thanked, which he did not mind; he could not be blamed either, and he found, with some surprise, that he minded that. He would be handing over the heaviest paper that he had ever carried, and he would have to do it as a man slides a bowl along a board, and look away.

He thought that it was probably what the boy would have done for him.

"Cael," said Karis's voice, behind him.

He heard the boy give a figure. He heard a pencil stop. *Again*, said Karis, quite gently; and then the boy's voice, a little too level, saying that he had done it twice, from the front and from the back.

"Then you are one short of me."

Seln turned round.

He had not thought of it. He had taken one sheet from the foot of one row, to keep a thing from being said before it was sure. He had forgotten that she never trusted a count until a second person had made it. She had put that row into the one pair of hands in which a cord had read short. He saw those hands now. They lay flat on the rock, on either side of the heap, and they had gone the colour of the paper. The boy was looking at them as a man looks at a tool that has failed him twice.

Seln had been wrong about a desk, after a hundred and forty-seven days of counting it. He had been wrong about this by the width of a slab of rock, and it had taken him less than an hour.

He crossed the turf, and made no more noise than he ever did. He set the sheet down between those two hands, with its head away from the boy, as every paper had lain before him in his life. He squared it with one finger. Then he went back to the water, and stood with his face to the mule. He counted the sound of the spring, since a man who has placed a thing does not watch it found.

He minded that a great deal more than he had allowed for.

---

It lay the wrong way up.

Cael saw that before he saw anything else, and for a breath it steadied him. A clerk who set a paper before a person turned it to face him. This one had been turned the other way, with its printed head toward the far side of the rock, as papers had lain on a registrar's counter at Denvash, and on a magister's desk, and on a table on a bluff. It was how he had learned to read. Somebody had noticed that, and had laid the sheet so on purpose.

He was not one short. That came next, like the blood coming back into a hand. There had been nothing the matter with his counting. A sheet had been out of the row, in a quiet man's sleeve, and here it was.

Then he understood that it had not been given back to the row.

He looked along the slab. Karis had sat down again on her stone. She had seen the sleeve, and she was writing something very small beside a figure, with her head bent; she did not look at him. Seln was at the basin with his back turned. Nobody else was nearer than Brom, who was frowning at the third row.

Cael read the sheet.

He had heard it read, by a lamp, in Lira's voice, from the far end of a table. There was the printed head. There were four short lines in a quick hand that leaned to the right, as a man writes on his knee. He knew what the first of them said, and the third, and where the fourth broke off. They had gone round in him for three days, with the stripe that lay across the rest.

The second line he had never seen. It was a date. There was a year of the registry's in it, and a day in that year, in a clerk's figures.

It told him nothing, at first. It was near a date that he knew, and it was not that date. He had read a great many, upside down, on a great many desks, and they were all of them near to one another. He would have left it there. But a man on a stick had said this morning that a return which came up from a station was dated in the stations' count, and had to be turned. He had said by how much.

It was not a hard sum. A child could have done it, the man had said.

He took off the difference.

He did it without moving his lips, with his two hands flat on the rock. He did not believe what he had, and he did it the other way about, from the far end, adding. He came back to the figures that lay before him in a stranger's ink.

He knew the day.

It was written in his own file, on the first line of the first sheet. It came after his name and before a word in square brackets, where the registry puts the day on which a person was classed. He had first read it at fourteen, the wrong way up, across a counter, while a woman with a pen made a small mark beside it. Every paper that the Compact had ever served on him had begun with it: a summons, and a challenge under a seal, and an order for a room and an hour. It was the day on which he had stood in a windowless building and put his hand where he was shown. A plate of brass had taken eleven seconds over him.

He had been waiting for it, he found, without knowing that he waited. A quiet man had stopped like a clock over that date, by a lamp. There was only one day in the registry's whole count that could have stopped him so on Cael's account.

*Deep layer*, the sheet said, in the first of its lines; and then two words; and then that day.

He stood very still.

He had two things. One was a line in a technician's hand, about something under a plate that nobody could open. The other was a date, and the date was his. He could feel the place where a verb would go between them. He could feel a dozen that would have fitted, each of them ready, as the ten were always ready, leaning toward the first stride.

He had learned from a small pale woman what a verb cost. She had sat up over one in a store behind a chimney, with a cord that read true and a hair in a fold of paper, and had refused to let it finish itself in her own head. He had not understood until now how hard that was.

He told it no.

A great many people were classed in a year. On that same morning, in other districts, other children had stood on other plates; a station's man who went below one, somewhere, might have found a thing beginning and written down the day. He did not know that it was his plate. He did not know what a sequence was, or what it meant for one to be running. The sheet did not say what it went with. It had begun to say, and somebody had laid a brush across the rest.

It was true, every word of it. It did not help at all, and he held to it regardless, as he would have held a rope.

His left hand had gone into his coat.

It had Hesk's book half out before he knew what it was about. He had written down everything that had ever happened to him. It was how an old man had taught him to keep from being told what he was: one noted the day, and the count, and the trend. He had the pencil. He had the place, under yesterday's line.

He looked at the blank beneath it for as long as he could have held his breath.

If he wrote the two days side by side, he would have drawn the line himself. It would not matter that he added no verb. A sum set down with its answer showing is a sentence. Anybody who opened that book would finish it for him: Lira, who had read it, or a healer, who had made him swear by it once. And it was not his alone. A man at a basin had carried it for three days, and had put it into his hands without a word, so that it need never be said.

He put the book back.

It was the first thing since he had learned to hold a pencil that he had known and had not entered. He was a little afraid of how easy it was.

He lifted the second row with both hands, and laid the technician's sheet in at its foot, where an office had filed it, and squared the heap upon it.

"It's whole," he said. "The row. It was only out for a while."

"I saw where it was." Karis had not looked up; and then she did, and her pale eyes went over his face once, carefully, as they went over a seal. "You read it."

"I've read it."

She had been given those words before, in a store, by another man, about the same sheet. He saw her hear them. She did not ask a second time. She wrote nothing, and tied the tape across the second row with a knot that she pulled very tight.

Lira was looking at him across the rock, with her chin up. She had a look for when he had put a thing away where she could not get at it, and he had seen it perhaps four times in his life. She gave it to him now, and held it, and let it go.

---

"That is all six," said Karis. "Counted twice, by two people, and agreed."

She had the rows back in the leather in their order, and the flap down. Nobody had spoken for some time. The sun had gone a little past its height, and the shadow of the outcrop had begun to creep out over the slab's western end.

Vastin had stood through the whole of the morning. He was standing now, a yard from the rock, with both hands on the head of his stick and his eyes on that flat of waxed leather. Cael, who had had reason to watch faces that day, saw something gather in his.

"There are five places," the old man said slowly. He was not speaking to anybody. "They have been measured for six years by an office that told no other office. They are classed, by a mark that the survey's own people carry on their iron. A surveyor who formed a view of them had the space for his view struck off the form, and is dead. And a return upon the deepest thing that the Compact owns has been laid among their sheets, for want of a shelf, with a brush drawn through it." His voice had quickened, and gone level. "This has to go up."

Nobody answered him.

He heard it then. Cael saw him hear it: the four words, in a voice that had said them from a desk every working day for forty years. He stood with his mouth a little open. His right hand had left the stick's head, and it hung in the air a foot from his coat, on its way to a pen.

"Up," he said, in another tone altogether. "To whom?"

It was Seln who spoke. He had come back from the basin when the tapes were tied, and had taken his place at the end of the slab, hands in his sleeves.

"You keep drafting orders."

"Habit."

"Out here the habit's the tell."

He let that lie for the length of a breath. He was not looking at the man. He was looking at the hand that hung in the air, as he had looked at it three times that morning and said nothing.

"Draft nothing. Just look."

Cael had known that voice for a long while. He had heard it give an inventory, and a warning, and a count of days; he had heard it refuse a good many things. He went back over all of that, standing by a rock on a moor, and he could not find one occasion on which it had told a person how a thing was done.

Seln kept doors. He had seen two posts a hand out of true, and had told one man, in eight words, and let him mend them. He had stood with his back to a shed through a whole cold term so that he could never be asked what was in it. He knew more of how to be overlooked than any of them; and when an Archmarshal had answered a hand aloud on a road, and stood like a gatepost at a ditch, it had been a girl who walked backward in front of him and beat it out. Seln had watched. He had not said a word.

He had said seven now. It was the first thing that he had ever taught anybody, in Cael's hearing. He had chosen for it the one man there who had once had the power to file him.

"The tell," said Vastin.

"A man who reaches for a pen that is not there has told the room what he was. So has one who classes a paper before he has read it, or says *it should be directed*." Seln's mild voice did not alter. "I knew you in a doorway for what you were before you had opened your mouth, and I was not the only person on this side of the Line who could have. There is a town at the end of that road. There is a gate in it, and a man in the gate who has been reading people for his living since before I entered the service. You will not need to tell him your name. You will only need to stand at his counter and begin to arrange it."

"And if I look?"

"Then you will see what is in front of you. It is all that the rest of us have ever had."

Vastin was silent. Cael watched him do the thing that he had been told, and it was a strange sight; the man had read rooms for forty years, in an order, giving each object its due, and he now had to look at one without doing anything to it. His right hand came down, and found the stick. His eyes went to the slab.

"What's there?" said Lira. She said it without any mischief.

"A courier's flat, strapped. A calfskin, with four stones on it." He was a long time over each. "A book that belonged to a dead man. An iron stake that has never been driven. Some pebbles, which that young woman has laid out by size." He stopped. "And a wind. I had not noticed until now that there was a wind."

"Good," said Seln.

He went to see to the mule's girth, which did not want seeing to. It was Brom who had the last of it, from the basin's rim, with his boot half on.

"He told you that for nothing," he said. "I'd keep it. He's not given any of us so much in a year."

---

Karis had not got up from her stone.

She sat with the flat across her knees and her two gloved hands laid on it. Cael had seen her so on the hundred and thirty-third night, with a strip of brass and a calfskin, going past the edge of what she had. She did not look tired. She looked like a person who has added a long column and is afraid to read the foot of it.

"Six years," Karis said. "They measured all of it. They graded it — the sites are ranked by something, and it's the same something in the kit, and I can't read either one."

She turned her head, and looked westward, where the road went over the next swell of the moor.

"Everything I can't read points at the same place."

[[TIER-B: "Treswick," / "The archive under it. Cleon." / "Who's Cleon?" — the plan gives these three lines without their speakers; coordinator to supply the lines and tags here]]

"The only historical-Path practitioner registered west of the Line. If the language is anywhere, it's under his hands. A day and a half."

She took one glove off, a finger at a time, and looked at the hand inside it.

"And then I find out whether I've been right for five years or wrong for five years, and I don't know which I'm more afraid of."

Nobody answered her at once.

It was the first time that the town had been named aloud since a page was opened on a table. It lay on the moor now among the seven of them, like a thing set down. Cael turned the other name over. It was a short one, and he had never heard it. A man sat under that town with his hands on a language, and had a name, and had been waiting there, it seemed, for somebody to bring him three floors.

At the basin a square woman had stopped wringing linen. She stood with a wet cloth in her two fists and her head a little turned. She had heard a station spoken of twice at a board, and had shut her mouth on something each time; she had heard an archive spoken of today. He saw her put the cloth down on the stones, very deliberately, as she put down a thing that she meant to come back to.

"If you're wrong," said Brom, "we've had a walk."

"If I am wrong, I have asked a person to go into the place that he fears most on the strength of fifty-two sheets and a feeling."

"He's not been asked," said Lira. "He took a slip." She had come round the slab, and she stood over the small pale woman with her left hand out of her pocket. "You said *ours* at that plank before any of us. I never heard you want a thing before. I'd sooner walk a day and a half behind somebody who wants something than sit at Lowmarch with somebody who's being careful."

"It is not a reason."

"No. It's a boot. Put the glove on; your hand's cold."

Karis put the glove on.

He went and got the mule himself. Nobody had asked for hands, and nobody did. There was a road, and it went one way. A job had put them on it, and what lay at its end had been named now by the person with the best right to name it. He found that he had no ruling left to give. He had only to pick up a box.

"The fork by dark," he said. "Oryn, how far is it?"

"Six miles," said the healer. She was drying her hands a finger at a time, and she did not look at him. "There's a fold there with a hearth in its corner, where a fire has walls round it. I'll have something to say when we're sat at it. I've put it off since a board at Lowmarch, and I've run out of road to put it off along."

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-19.md

# Chapter 19 — Different Verbs

The fork-stone stood where the ruts parted, a slab of the dark rock as tall as Brom, set on end by people who had left no other mark on that moor.

They came to it at dusk on the two hundred and eightieth day. One pair of ruts went on past it to the west, and began at once to climb a long bare rise that shut out the sky on that side. The other turned north, along the moor's edge, toward the blue hills. Somebody had cut a hand into each face of the stone, with the fingers pointing, and a hundred cold terms had worn both hands nearly smooth.

The fold lay a little way down the northern branch, in a dip. It was a drovers' pen, round, of dry stone laid shoulder-high without a grain of mortar, and three parts of it were open to the sky. The fourth had been roofed with slabs. In that corner there was a hearth built into the wall, with a flue, and beside the hearth a stack of cut peat as high as a man's waist.

"You burn what you want, and you cut as much again before you go, from the hag behind," said Oryn. "It's been the rule here since before the stone was stood up. A fire with a wall round it and a roof on it isn't on a road. I've slept here on thirty rounds."

Brom built it, laying the peats on end as she showed him, and Karis set one point among them without being asked; and the thing caught slowly, with a sour brown smell that Cael had never met. It was the first flame that the seven of them had sat by since a hold on a shoulder, and only the second since the river.

They did not all sit. Vastin stood in the gap of the fold, where the hurdle went, with his back against the stone and the bound leg out before him. He had come six miles from the spring on that stick, at the tail of the column, and had been offered a mule twice; and he had the grey look of a man who is counting what is left in him and finding it sufficient by a very little.

Seven bowls went round, and the mule had the open part of the pen to herself, where she stood with her rump to the wind.

Oryn ate standing, at her stride and a half, and when her bowl was empty she set it down on the wall's top and pushed neither sleeve up.

"I'm not an archive person," she said.

It was said to the fire, and nobody had asked her anything.

"That's the head of it, and I'll give you the rest in order, since I've had long enough to arrange it. You're going under a town to read. I've nothing to do down there. I can't read a stone; I'd not know a stratum from a stair. I mend what's put in front of me, and then I walk to the next. So you'll not be thinking that I'm coming, any of you, and I'll not have to watch you not ask."

"Nobody'd thought it," said Brom.

"You laid seven bowls."

"I've laid a bowl for you since a heap of stones. I never thought that it meant you'd go down a hole."

She looked at him, and the corner of her mouth went down.

"There's a thing that none of you has asked me in two hundred and eighty days, and I've been glad of it. I'll say it once, since it bears on what I want of him." She did not look at Cael. "I wasn't made out here. I was made in a city, inside the Line. It doesn't matter which; they're all one city, with different rivers. There was a house there for the sick, with forty beds and six of us to tend them, and I'd my rank from it."

"Iron," said Lira. "The fourth."

"Provisional. I never stayed to have it made anything else." She rubbed her palms together slowly. "Every bed in that house had a sheet on a board at its foot. There was the name on it, and the complaint, and what had been spent. And there was a column for the patient's tier."

"For the patient's," said Karis. Her pencil was not in her hand.

"Not for the healer's. For theirs. I filled that column in for a long while before I asked what it was for; I thought that it was like the name. Then there was a week of fever in the low town, and the house was full to the stairs, and I found out." She spoke as she spoke a finding. "We'd so much in us, the six of us together, as I've so much in me tonight, and it had to be spent on somebody. It went down the column from the top. There was a Gold in a bed by the window with a wrenched wrist. He had what he wanted on the first day. Then the Silvers had theirs."

Nobody at the fire moved.

"There was a woman brought in from the river sheds on the third night. She'd no tier that anybody could find. She'd never been stood on a plate, or her paper was lost; I never knew which. So there was nothing to put in the column." Oryn's eyes had gone to the peats. "A sheet with an empty column goes to the bottom of the heap. It's not cruelty. It's only how a heap is sorted."

"Did she die?" said Lira, very low.

"She did not. I'll not have you think it worse than it was. I went to her at night, off the sheet, with what I had left, and it was enough. And in the morning the woman who kept that house showed me the board, where there was a spending and no tier written against it. She said that a healer who spends off the sheet has stolen from whoever stood next on it." Her spoon was in her hand, and she turned it over. "She was right. That's the thing that I've never got past. By that house's sums she was right, and I couldn't fault a line of them. So I went to look for some other sums."

"You left," said Cael.

"I walked out of a gate in the coat that I stood in, and I've not been through one since."

---

She let them have that for the space of three breaths, and Cael did not need it explained. He had been a sheet with an empty column since his Kindling; and he understood now why a healer who had never asked him for a tier had once looked at his hand, on a mountain, as a person looks at a shut press.

"So I know what leaving is," said Oryn. "I've done it the once. It's when you go and you don't mean to come back to it, and you don't tell them where you'll be. I'll not have this mistaken for that."

She had turned from the fire, and her face was as level as he had ever seen it, and a good deal less tired than it had been at the cut.

"Leaving and routing are different verbs. I'm doing the second."

"Say the route," said Brom. He had his thick fingers ready, to count it on.

"North by that rut at first light. There are five gates up along the hill-foot that I've not seen since before the snow, and one of them has a child in it that I was to have heard a cold term ago. Then down the far water to the ford, and over, and on to the mill from above. It's my own round, walked the wrong way about. The mill has waited all the cold for me, and it'll wait as long again and say that I'm late." Her eyes went to the small pale woman on the meal-sack. "How long does a person want, to read a hole under a town?"

"I cannot tell you. I have never been let into one."

"Three weeks," said Seln.

He had said it to the peats, and everybody at that fire turned to him, since he did not give figures that he had not counted.

"A provision of that kind is granted by the week. I have seen the form. In the first week the keeper of a place decides whether he believes what he has been shown. In the second there is work. And nobody who has waited years for a thing has ever left it at the end of a second week." His hands were in his sleeves. "I should not put my name to it. I should be surprised to be wrong by more than four days."

"Three weeks is five gates and a ford, at my pace, with a day in hand," said Oryn. "That'll put me at Millrace. I'll be at the mill's bench when you come down off that road, and if you're late I'll hear of it from a carter."

"And if you're late?" said Lira.

"Then somebody's needed me worse than you do, and you'll wait."

She came round the fire then. She stopped at her stride and a half from Cael, and he saw her mouth open; and he knew the sentence before it came, not by its words but by its shape, since he had watched it arrive in her three times and be shut in.

This time she did not shut it.

"You said a station, at that board. He said it first, by the fire, and then with a bowl turned over. I know stations. There's one on the crown of every city inside the Line, with steps going up to it and a square in front, and I've stood in such a square with a line of children and watched them go up one at a time." She did not push up her sleeves. "It's the one thing in a town that's built to read whatever is brought near it. And I've had my hands on you, a long while back, and I found a thing that I made you write down and leave alone. I've not touched it since. I don't know what it is."

"Nor do I," said Cael.

"No. So I don't know what a station does with it. That's the whole of my reason, and it's no better than that. I've no finding. I've a thing that I can't read, and a thing that reads, and I'd not walk any patient of mine past the one with the other in him until I knew." Her voice had not risen. "You go inside, you don't go near the station. Not the steps, not the square. You promise me in that log of yours, because I've seen what the log does to you."

Nobody spoke, and Karis had lowered her head. Vastin, in the gap, was looking at the healer with an attention that Cael could not read; he had certified those stations, and he said nothing.

Cael did not ask her what she meant about the log, since he knew. A thing that he had only said, he could weigh again in the morning. A thing that he had written in Hesk's book was done; he had kept a count of uses for a whole cold term because a small pale woman had seen him write the number.

It was what she was asking for. She did not want his word; she wanted the one form of it that he had never broken.

He took the book out of his coat, and opened it on his knee, toward the fire. He set the day at the head, since he could not write anything without it. Then he wrote what he had been asked for, and it took him a good deal longer than the length of it deserved.

"Read it," said Oryn.

He read it to her, aloud, with the peat-smoke in his eyes.

*The fork. Oryn north; five holds; routing, not the other verb. Her term, given at the fire, taken in the log because that's the form she'll take it in: inside Treswick I do not go near the station. Not the steps. Not the square. Written here so she can read it, and so I can.*

He turned the book round, and held it out to her across the stride and a half.

She did not take it; she bent, with her hands on her knees, and read it where it lay in his, from the day to the last word, with her lips moving. He had written *inside*, and he had not seen that until he heard himself say it. There was no slip any longer between himself and that word; he had put it down in pencil, in the plain hand, as a place where he would be.

"That'll do," said Oryn. She straightened. "I can read that. I couldn't have read a promise."

---

She priced them after that, one at a time, while the fire was good.

It was what she did at every gate before she left it, and Cael had seen it done at a mill and at a heap of stones. She did not sit to it. She went round the fold at her own distance from each, and asked before she put out a hand, and said what she found to the person and to nobody else; but the fold was small, and all of them heard all of it.

"Arm," she said to Lira. "May I?"

"Yes."

The thick fingers went round a forearm that was no longer quite as thin as a rail. Her lips moved to ten.

"Fifteen days. It held a hound yesterday, by what I'm told, and it's none the worse. It's slow, and it'll be slow for as long again. They've stone floors where you're going, and stairs. Don't fall on it."

"I've been not falling on it for fifteen days."

"Then you've the habit."

Brom had his boot off before she reached him.

"That's a foot. Ten days, I said, and it's nine now. The shoulder's another woman's knot, and I can't read through it, and she's the far side of a river. You'll be a month and more from her." She put one finger on the drover's turn, as the old woman had, and took it away. "If it tells you anything, you put down whatever you're carrying. You don't undo that. You find somebody with a bench."

"There'll be none that'll touch me in there. I'm Copper in a book."

"Then you'll put the thing down and leave it down, which is harder." She had gone on to the meal-sack already. "You, I can find nothing the matter with. You've not slept a night through since a ferry, and you'll go down under a town and forget that people eat. I can't mend that. Somebody's to put a bowl in front of her and stand there."

"I'll stand there," said Brom.

"I know that you will."

She stopped before the sill of the hearth, where a small grey man sat with a case on his chest.

"You've never had my hands on you, and you've never offered, and I'll not ask; you'd say no, and I'd sooner not make you say it." Seln inclined his head by a very little. "I'll price what I can see from here. You've carried that on the one shoulder for longer than I've known you, and it's pulled you a finger out of true. Change shoulders."

"I shall consider it."

"That's a no in a clean shirt."

She crossed the fold to the gap.

Cael watched her do it, and she did not go any quicker than she had gone to the others, or any slower, and she stopped at the same distance.

"Knee. May I?"

"Yes," said Vastin.

She went down on her heels, and ran two fingers under the binding's upper edge, and under the lower, and pressed with her thumb beside the joint while she looked at his face and not at her thumb.

"This is the first day. There are twenty-nine after it. It's stood a spring and six miles of road, and it's no worse than I left it, which is more than you deserved." She sat back. "Stick; binding; no kneeling. No walls. You'll want to carry something tomorrow, when those boxes come off my mule. You're not to."

"I shall take that under advisement."

"You'll take it under your arm, with the stick." But she had not said it sharply. She had given him as long as she had given a girl's arm, to the breath, and he had marked it; Cael saw him look after her as she rose, as a man looks who has been handed something across a counter and can find no price on it.
